#208. India: Delhi chokes on most polluted Diwali eve in 10 years

Delhi was ranked as the world's most polluted major city on the eve of Diwali. Delhi's air pollution is expected to get worse in the coming days.

This blog is now closed. It was a roundup of headlines from across India on Saturday, November 7, and Sunday, November 8:

And that's a wrap! We will be back tomorrow.

The Delhi government used less than half of its budget under the National Tuberculosis Elimination Programme (NTEP) last year, according to official records.

Delhi spent only 47.4% of the total funds allocated in 2025 to fight Tuberculosis.

Only Rs 51.32 crore (about €4.9 million) of the Rs 108.27 crore approved under the NTEP was utilised, leaving Rs 56.95 crore unspent, according to the Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS) report of 2025-26.

The funds were underused even though 112,977 TB cases were notified in 2025 in the capital, according to the Delhi State Health Mission (DSHS).

Explaining the discrepancy, a Ministry of Health official told The Indian Express that under the program, beneficiaries receive funds directly and "gaps in digitisation and beneficiary identification contributed to this shortfall in fund utilisation."

Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi on Sunday joined security forces deployed in the central state of Chhattisgarh to celebrate Diwali.

The state has long been affected by a Maoist-inspired Naxal insurgency.

Celebrating Diwali with security forces in Chhattisgarh's Bastar, Modi said, "Because of your sacrifices, we have put an end to the terror of Naxalism here."

"This is the first Diwali where every single district in the country is free from Naxalism. This is the first Diwali where crores of our citizens have overcome the fear of Naxalism," he said.

Modi made an indirect reference to the youth protests that took place in the past few weeks demanding the removal of election chief Gyanesh Kumar.

Modi called the protesters "Dimagi naxals" (Intellectual naxals) and said, "While armed Naxalites have been wiped out from the forests, 'Dimagi Naxals' have taken control of several organisations."

"Even today, they are inciting people against the country's Constitution and democracy. We must identify them, defeat them and ensure the security of the nation," he said.

Modi's remarks come after his government announced in March that the country has been freed from Naxal groups after several Maoist leaders and cadres were killed in operations by India's security forces.

Activist Sonam Wangchuk has said he is the subject of a witch-hunt by the Ladakh Lieutenant Governor's office, which he accused of targeting him in a smear campaign for standing with the youth of the country and participating in protests.

"I am being continuously witch-hunted and defamed and our schools are being harassed in every possible way by the LG administration in Ladakh," said Wangchuk, who is a member of Leh Apex Body (LAB), a representative body of the people of Ladakh.

In a video message on Saturday to Indian President Droupadi Murmu, Wangchuk asked her to intervene, saying, "Madam President, for the last six years. We don't have rights like in the rest of the country or democracy."

In 2019, Ladakh lost the special status it shared with the former state of Jammu and Kashmir under Article 370 and became a separate Union Territory. The change meant that the Indian central government in New Delhi now directly governs the Himalayan region.

Since 2019, several political and civil society groups in Ladakh have been demanding greater autonomy and, in many cases, statehood.

Wangchuk said two social media pages, Ladakh Lens 1 and Ladakh Vision 1, surfaced on October 14 and that the former was allegedly created to defame him through "unfounded allegations," while the latter was aimed at "praising the Lieutenant Governor's work" in Ladakh.

The Ladakh administration has not responded to the allegations so far.

Wangchuk also accused the Ladakh administration of targeting institutes like his that have "earned national and international repute."

Earlier this month, the Ladakh administration directed the Himalayan Institute of Alternative Learning (HIAL), led by Wangchuk and his wife Gitanjali J. Angmo, to halt activities at its Phyang-based school until the Jammu and Kashmir and Ladakh High Court issues further notice.

Wangchuk has questioned the approvals being demanded by the administration.

Delhi awoke Sunday morning enveloped in smog with poor visibility after the previous night saw the worst air quality for Diwali eve in 10 years.

India's Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB) said the Indian capital had a "very poor" air quality index (AQI) of 362 on Saturday. The worst polluted pre-Diwali day was in 2016, when the AQI hit 404 on October 29, according to the CPCB.

The air quality is forecast to turn "severe" on Sunday, with Diwali celebrations adding to the pollution.

Swiss monitor IQAir, meanwhile, ranked Delhi as the world's worst polluted major city on Saturday.

While vehicular emissions and stubble burning in neighboring states of Punjab and Haryana are major contributors to air pollution, Diwali celebrations, which involve large-scale burning of firecrackers, add to the pollution in the city.

The government had already ordered schools in and ​around Delhi to offer online classes, and ​encouraged people to work from home ahead of ⁠the Hindu festival of light.

Sanitation workers in state capital Bhopal ended their indefinite strike after three days following late-night talks with Chief Minister Mohan Yadav, according to Indian news agency Press Trust of India.

According to the sanitation workers' union, the breakthrough came after the state government agreed to initiate the regularization process for workers employed for 25 days a month, a move which will allow a pay hike.

According to local media, the sanitation workers had four major demands: a pay rise, compassionate appointments, regularization of jobs and an end to the short-term gig worker contract system. Their primary demand is an increase in the monthly wage from Rs 10,500 (about $111) to Rs 25,000.

The protest had left Bhopal, which was named the cleanest state capital in India in 2023, littered with heaps of uncollected garbage.

The workers claimed that more than 100,000 workers participated in the agitation across Madhya Pradesh.

One of India’s most successful fast bowlers, Mohammed Shami, has announced his retirement from Test cricket.

The move brings his 13-year career to an end, but he will continue to play white ball formats.

The 36-year-old cricketer announced in an Instagram video that he was ending his career in whites.

"I am bidding farewell to the white jersey, but never to the memories associated with it. When I wore India's Test jersey for the first time, I had only one dream — to do something for my country. Since then, every wicket, every struggle, every injury, and every comeback has taught me something," Shami said.

Shami made his Test debut against the West Indies at the Eden Gardens on November 6, 2013. He finished with 229 wickets at an average of 27.71, including six five-wicket hauls.

His international red ball journey spans 229 wickets from 64 matches, making him the sixth most successful pacer in Tests for the country behind Kapil Dev (434), Zaheer Khan, Ishant Sharma (both 311), Javagal Srinath (236), Jasprit Bumrah (234).

Shami last played for India in Tests in the World Test Championship final against Australia at The Oval in 2023.

Since then, injuries have disrupted his career, but he remained an important part of India's white-ball setup.

Shami clarified that his retirement would not mark the end of his playing career. He said he would continue to play white-ball cricket and could also turn out in four-day matches occasionally.

Social media platform X said that police orders it received to remove online accounts of two of India's most ​prominent free speech groups were fake.

Lawyers representing X told the court that someone impersonating Delhi police had sent the emails.

X received orders through its online reporting form in the name of a ​senior Delhi police officer seeking removal of the online accounts of free speech groups, the Internet Freedom Foundation (IFF) and the Software Freedom Law Centre, ⁠India (SFLC.in).

The orders claimed the groups had defamed the Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh, parent organization of Prime Minister Narendra Modi's Hindu nationalist Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP).

After an internal investigation, the social media company's lawyers on Friday told the Delhi High Court that, "It ​turns out this was somebody playing an impostor, somebody impersonating Delhi police who has sent this."

The orders had sparked an uproar on social media this week and were challenged for their legality by Amnesty and India's main opposition party.

The Supreme Court of India on Friday said that vehicular pollution is a major contributor to the deteriorating air quality in Delhi and questioned the effectiveness of the government's measures to curb it.

The bench, comprising Chief Justice of India (CJI) Surya Kant and Justices Joymalya Bagchi and V Mohana, opened the proceedings with a jibe saying, "I hope you all are enjoying the weather in Delhi."

This week, Delhi's Air Quality Index (AQI) has remained above the "hazardous" 300 mark.

Air quality in the capital regularly plummets at the outset of winter, as weather conditions help trap pollutants.

"A major cause of the pollution is vehicular pollution. Look at the roads in Delhi, look at the roads connecting Delhi to the NCR (the National Capital Region)… the kind of bumper-to-bumper traffic… that's the major cause," the CJI said.

The court examined the steps taken by the government to control the rising levels of air pollution.

Expressing concerns over the failure of measures to curb air pollution in Delhi over the years, the court said, "To think we can achieve air quality of less than 100 AQI in Delhi during winters is utopian when 'very poor' and 'severe' have become the new normal."

On stubble burning, the court said farmers should be given economic incentives and practical alternatives.

The court also encouraged responsible celebrations during festivals, particularly Diwali, where firecrackers being set off adds to the air pollution.

"Why don't we revert to our original customs?" the CJI said, adding celebrations should be accompanied by a sense of responsibility towards the environment.

#207. Trump tells 60 Minutes that deportation policy was ‘too tough’

President makes unusual acknowledgement that one of the defining policies of his second administration had become a political liability

Donald Trump has admitted that his deportation policy was “too tough” and has offered to strike an immigration deal with Democrats in a concession following the Republicans’ disastrous defeat in the midterm elections.

In an interview on the CBS network’s 60 Minutes broadcast on Sunday, the president acknowledged that the aggressive crackdown had hurt him politically and said he was open to an arrangement to allow some undocumented immigrants to remain in the US.

Trump was asked by 60 Minutes correspondent Ross Douthat whether his deportation policy had gone too far. “I think it was too tough,” he said. “I think it might be too tough, yeah. And a lot of people would say that it’s great. But a lot of people think it’s too tough.”

The president’s comments offered an unusually frank acknowledgment that one of the defining policies of his second administration had become a political liability. But they were seemingly at odds with his decision on Friday to elevate Stephen Miller, the architect of his immigration crackdown, to White House chief of staff.

Since Trump returned to office, sweeping Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) raids at workplaces, homes and public spaces have ensnared people with no criminal records, separated families and spread fear in immigrant communities. Federal agents fatally shot two US citizens, Renee Good and Alex Pretti, during operations in Minneapolis earlier this year.

The president claimed that his policy “both helped and hurt”, since people gave him an “A+ for closing up the border” and “very high marks for getting the bad ones out”. But he admitted: “I get hurt because we also took people that had good jobs, that created one violation, or breaking of the law, and that was that they came into the country illegally.”

Trump added that there were competing views within the country about whether undocumented immigrants should be deported or allowed to remain if they were working and paying taxes. He said some people believed that “they should be allowed to stay if they’re good people working in a landscaping company” and “in some cases, paying taxes”.

Trump said he believed an agreement with Democrats was achievable, allowing some long-term undocumented residents to stay while continuing to remove people convicted of serious crimes. He proposed targeting killers, rapists and drug dealers while making an accommodation for people who had been in the country for 10 or 15 years.

“Let the people that are here, and in some cases here for 10 and 15 years, work a deal for them,” he said. He described the proposal as something that could be negotiated, saying: “I think that’s something that’s actually doable.”

Trump suggested Democrats were receptive to a compromise, saying some had already contacted him. Asked whether he could refrain from name-calling for six months to secure an agreement, he replied: “Very easily.”

Trump also blamed the war with Iran and higher petrol prices for much of the Republicans’ midterms defeat, agreeing with an assessment by his vice-president, JD Vance, that the conflict had been a major factor. He also cited what he described as growing dislike of Israel.

He maintained, however, that the military campaign had been justified and said he would make the same decision again. “My answer is an emphatic yes because you can’t let Iran have a nuclear weapon,” he said.

Trump insisted the war had been won and predicted that Tehran would eventually negotiate a deal. He acknowledged that the military campaign might not have been popular in the short term, saying: “I think historically, this will be a very good decision. Is it a good decision for today and tomorrow? Maybe not.”

He said he was not looking to escalate the conflict after the midterms.

On the economy, the president conceded that his administration had failed to communicate its achievements effectively, despite repeatedly claiming that employment, investment and stock market figures were at record levels. “I think it was bad messaging, maybe by me,” he said.

Trump signalled an openness to safeguards for artificial intelligence, despite having previously favoured allowing technology companies to police themselves rather than imposing sweeping government restrictions. “I don’t mind guardrails,” he told 60 Minutes. “But I don’t wanna put us in a deep second position.”

The interview also raised questions about the substantial increase in Trump’s personal wealth since his return to the White House.

Douthat cited a Forbes estimate that Trump’s net worth had increased by $3bn from the autumn of 2024 to the autumn of 2025. Trump initially suggested that the increase reflected the general performance of the stock market and said his finances were managed by his son.

Asked about his family’s cryptocurrency business, World Liberty Financial, in which he is a co-owner and which has attracted investment from people connected to the United Arab Emirates, Trump defended the arrangement by saying: “I know nothing about it.”

But Dan Alexander, a Forbes senior editor who calculates the net worth of wealthy individuals for the magazine, challenged Trump’s explanation in a lengthy thread on X, arguing that the president’s increased fortune could not be explained simply by stock market gains.

Alexander identified several sources including cryptocurrency ventures that generated more than $1bn in proceeds and the appreciation of tokens retained by Trump, overseas licensing agreements that expanded the value of his branding business, and the removal of an almost $500m financial penalty in his New York civil fraud case.

Trump also offered a glimpse of the Republican succession battle ahead of the 2028 presidential election, saying he might endorse a candidate but had not decided whether to intervene. Asked whether he expected to endorse a successor, Trump replied: “Probably. Not necessarily.”

He described the Republican party as having a strong field of potential candidates and praised secretary of state Marco Rubio while declining to rule out other contenders.

The president said his endorsement would carry considerable weight but suggested he might be better off staying out of the contest. He also expressed confidence that the “Make America great again” movement could survive without him as its leader, describing its defining principles as strong borders and a strong military.

At the end of the interview, Trump was asked whether he could forgive political opponents including former FBI director James Comey and former House speaker Nancy Pelosi, even before the judgment of God. Trump said: “The answer is no. I want to be honest, no.”

Douthat followed up: “Mr President. You have had the most charmed and remarkable life of any American in my entire lifetime and you are telling me that before the judgment throne of God you couldn’t find it in your heart to forgive some of your enemies? After all that’s been given to you? Come on. Help me out here.”

But Trump remained adamant, insisting: “I probably cannot do that, no, because I do hate some of my enemies and I always will.”

Douthat then noted that many presidents are remembered with a single line such as “He freed the slaves” or “He won the cold war” and asked what line will Trump’s presidency be remembered with.

The president said simply: “That I made America great again.”

#205. Coordinated disturbances haunt grids worldwide. Here’s how researchers solved the mystery.

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

For years, mysterious electrical anomalies have been flummoxing grid managers around the world. Every 60 seconds, the frequency on the grid would drop a tiny bit.

Even weirder: The sags happened at the exact same time on power grids across countries and continents.

It was a mystery that Dirk Witthaut, a researcher at Forschungszentrum Jülich in Germany, couldn’t pass up.

Germany’s largest transmission system operator, Tennet Germany, brought the puzzle to Witthaut, and it took years of investigative and modeling work to determine the cause. From Europe to Australia, the U.S. to Malaysia, the same pattern shows up in the data.

Such frequency drops aren’t unheard of, but they are troubling. And the regularity with which they have been occurring can make the phenomenon feel even more ominous.

The grid’s orderly operation relies on the frequency being kept within a certain range. If the oscillations get get too large, or if grid operators don’t know the cause and respond improperly, they can lead to blackouts. Even if the sags don’t cause blackouts, they can damage equipment in power plants if they go unchecked, especially in hydropower turbines.

Hydropower plants have historically been a main driver of so-called ultra-low frequency oscillations (UFLOs). Every turbine vibrates, and if those vibrations occur with a certain regularity, known as the resonant frequency, they can magnify, sometimes to dangerous levels. Think of a wine glass breaking before an opera singer’s mighty pipes — that’s resonance.

So Witthaut and his team started investigating hydropower, the most obvious potential culprit. They used a computer model of a hydropower plant control system on the Scandinavian grid to test the hypothesis.

While they managed to find conditions that could generate a resonance that peaked once per minute, they couldn’t explain why it persisted for so long. Typically, resonances in mechanical systems will come and go, and so will the accompanying signal on the electrical grid. But this frequency drop kept happening minute after minute after minute. Every 60 seconds, without fail.

It then dawned on the team that anything operating with such clockwork required, well, a clock. How else could they explain the uncanny timing?

To that hypothesis, the researchers started close to home, installing more than 80 smart power quality meters around the Jülich campus. The research site is outfitted with medium- and low-voltage distribution lines, transformers, solar panels, and battery energy storage systems that serve as uninterruptible power supplies (UPS). It also has a high-performance computer system as well as the usual office computers powered by much smaller UPS devices. The power quality meters sampled a range of attributes, including frequency, voltage, current, and power, once every three seconds.

Before long, the research team had its answer. The measurements recorded on the transformer attached to the battery energy storage system peaked every minute, just like on the bigger power grids.

It turns out that many stationary batteries, from data center UPSes to large grid-scale batteries, check their state-of-charge every minute. Since these devices are internet-connected, their clocks are in near perfect synchronicity. Every time one checks its charge, the disturbance is imperceptible on the wide grid. But when they all do it together, that disruption becomes apparent.

To find evidence that UPSes were the main culprit on the grid, Witthaut and his team turned to data provided by grid operators. They found their answer in Ireland.

There, data centers have grown to consume a large fraction of the country’s electricity. By 2022, data centers represented 18% of the load on Ireland’s grid, and each facility is served by a fleet of UPSes. Sure enough, between 2016 and 2021, the minute-by-minute frequency drop on the Irish grid quadrupled from 3 milli-Hertz (mHz) in 2016 to 12 mHz in 2021. More recently, some drops have exceeded 20 mHz.

Ireland’s 50 Hz grid is built to withstand swings of plus or minus 200 mHz, though the grid operator would rather limit the swings to 100 mHz. In that range, 20 mHz isn’t a lot, but data centers aren’t the only loads on the grid. If other loads also cause the frequency to sag, the extra bump from every UPS checking its state-of-charge could push the grid toward blackout conditions.

The problem is entirely preventable. Rather than check state-of-charge exactly on the minute, Witthaus and team recommend that UPS and grid-scale battery manufacturers add some randomization to the schedule.

While UPSes are driving the problem today, there’s a risk that the phenomenon could persist. The researchers also tested IoT devices. In devices as diverse as a smart oven and Raspberry Pi clusters, they were able to detect periodic power fluctuations. The fix is also a simple software patch, but many IoT devices go unpatched. A recent study found that less than 3% of IoT devices are running the latest firmware.

Regulators may soon have to step in to demand changes, but they can only address their corner of the grid. Given the global nature of the issue, it’ll ultimately require global coordination. The mystery may be solved, but the problem could remain for years to come.

#203. Poland begins two days of mourning over Krakow shooting

Polish authorities have opened a terror probe after the shooting on Krakow's Main Square. Police confirmed that the bodies found at the suspect's home were those of his parents and sister.

Poland began two days of mourning on Sunday, a day after a gunman killed six people and wounded 15 others in Krakow's main market square.

The attack occurred in Main Square (Rynek Glowny) in the Old Town of Poland's second-largest city on Saturday. The area is a popular tourist attraction.

In addition to the six people killed in the square, three bodies found at the suspect's home were the bodies of his sister, mother and father, police said Sunday.

Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk has labeled the attack an act of far-right "terrorism," but authorities have not given a possible motive.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Poland's national prosecutor's office said it had launched an investigation into Saturday's shooting, saying that it constituted a suspected "terrorist offense."

Mourners left candles in the square to pay tribute to the victims of the shooting. Black flags and Polish national flags with black ribbons were also seen in the city.

The square remained partially sealed off on Sunday morning as authorities continued investigations.

The 24-year-old suspect, who was wearing military fatigues at the time of the attack, allegedly shot at people at random as he drove around the square.

He was wounded by police and is in critical condition in hospital.

Polish authorities say the man held right-wing extremist and ultranationalist views.

"Everything indicates that he was fascinated by far-right ideologies," Interior Minister Marcin Kierwinski said at a news conference.

A British national was among the dead, and among the wounded were citizens of Austria, the United Kingdom, Germany, Portugal and Russia, according to authorities.

Two police officers were wounded while confronting the suspect and are in hospital. Officials have launched an appeal for blood donations for one of the officers, who is in critical condition.

The Krakow Carriage Driver's Association told Polish news agency PAP that several horse-drawn carriage drivers were seriously injured in the shooting.

Police said on Sunday that three bodies found at the suspect's home were those of his sister, mother and father.

The family home is in the town of Ostrowiec Swietokrzyski, around 150 kilometers (93 miles) northeast of Krakow.

Police said that several firearms were seized from the apartment.

The suspect had no previous criminal record, according to Polish media reports.

He and his father were members of a shooting club.

Japan’s rural depopulation is reshaping farmland and forests in ways that can reduce biodiversity. A 2025 study found that in areas where population declined, butterflies, fireflies, frogs, birds, and native plants also became less abundant, suggesting that some species depend on the human-managed mosaic landscapes created by small farms.

#201. If Snap’s Specs are still ridiculously expensive, they’re also a lot more comfortable than they used to be

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

When I tried a prototype of Snap’s Specs at CES in Las Vegas earlier this year, the experience left something to be desired. The company’s AR glasses — which the company calls a “new kind of computer” — were heavy (they weighed around 8 ounces) and fit rather uncomfortably on my face. The device was also hot, and made my face feel tingly — a common side-effect of smart glasses where massive computing is happening under the hood.

Fast forward to late October, when I met back up with the Snap team at a Four Seasons in downtown San Francisco. By this time, the company had debuted Specs publicly during an event with CEO Evan Spiegel, and pre-orders of the company’s long-gestating headset were already underway.

What I can say is this: Specs has improved the form factor significantly since my first brush with the device. The glasses now weigh nearly half of what the previous version did, slimming down to 132 grams, or approximately 4.6 ounces. They were quite comfortable and, during the hour-ish that I demo-ed the device, it did not heat up like the previous version had.

Specs now comes in two sizes — 47mm and 52mm. The purpose here is purely to accommodate different head sizes, ensuring the glasses fit snugly on whoever’s face happens to be wearing them. They also now come equipped with spring hinges, which make the device more flexible and comfortable.

However, if the body of the device has improved greatly, its soul is still a work-in-progress. The glasses can be fun — but they also still seem to lack a dedicated purpose, and one struggles to imagine how, specifically, this expensive head gear ($2,200 a pop) would prove useful in a daily capacity. When I asked Snap staff what the device was really for — fun and games or productivity — the answer was vague. The device attempts to be a bunch of things at once and, in the process, ends up feeling a little rudderless.

I have racked my brain to come up with some daily use-cases for Specs. Some of the things I came up with were:

By contrast, here are some of the areas I’m still not totally sold on as Specs use-cases:

Specs is impressive for how the Snap team has managed to integrate a variety of user experiences into one unified device. There are a lot of impressive achievements here — specifically the spatial synching tech that allows two users to see the same thing simultaneously — that, with some further development, could be deployed towards some really interesting use cases.

The bottom line is that Snap’s device can continue to grow and transform as the company iterates. It’s an interesting product that — like a lot of its peers — doesn’t necessarily seem to have a clear purpose but, in time, could become many things.

What the device currently offers, however, may not be compelling enough to encourage people to shell out the cash that Snap is asking for.

#200. Riot breaks out at influencer boxing event attended by Tommy Robinson in Ireland

Two people arrested after Robinson’s presence inflames tensions in scenes witnessed by many on social media

Two people were arrested at an influencer boxing event that descended into a riot in Ireland after the presence of the far-right activist Tommy Robinson inflamed tensions in the crowd.

The riot took place at about midnight on Saturday at an event organised by the controversial internet personality HSTikkyTokky, whose real name is Harrison Sullivan, at the Hub events venue in Kilkenny.

Robinson, 43, is on bail after being found guilty in October of harassing two journalists to the point it caused them fear of violence. He is due back in court on 16 December for a sentencing hearing, with a substantial custodial sentence possible.

Robinson, whose real name is Stephen Yaxley-Lennon, was a ringside guest and surrounded by a number of his supporters at the event, which was organised by Sullivan and fellow internet personality Ed Matthews under their Unhinged events company. Sullivan and Matthews earlier this year featured in a Netflix documentary about manosphere influencers. On Friday, Sullivan told the Guardian that Robinson would be attending the event.

A chaotic and violent press conference on Friday evening was a sign of the disorder to come, with competitors repeatedly attacking each other. They included streamers, fitness personalities, drill rappers, OnlyFans models, and Paul Flaherty, a convicted sex offender turned YouTuber.

The event, which was livestreamed on the Kick platform to about 1.8 million viewers, began without incident. The atmosphere grew tense when Sullivan and Matthews invited Robinson to the commentator’s desk before a fight by the Muslim social media personality Mo Deen, whose real name is Mohammad Kubba.

Robinson, who was holding a copy of his book The Grooming Gang Cover-Up, told Matthews that “Muslims have been raping our country for 100 years”, and cited false statistics about Muslim men and sexual offences. Many of the racially diverse people at the event took offence at Robinson’s comments.

After Kubba won his fight he climbed out of the ring and jumped on to Robinson, throwing punches. A scuffle was brought under control by security, with further competitive fights set to resume.

However, the event erupted into a riot after Sullivan demanded that Kubba, who had left the event, return to “face off” against Robinson in the ring. A reluctant Kubba was escorted back into the venue by the event’s security team. It appeared that Sullivan wished for the pair to have an unscheduled bareknuckle fight, with Sullivan repeatedly heard saying “let Tommy fight him”. Sullivan also allowed Robinson’s entourage, whom he referred to as “Tommy’s boys”, into the ring.

Upon Kubba’s arrival in the ring, which by now was filled with Robinson’s supporters, Robinson threw a copy of his book, which has Arabic-style lettering, at Kubba, and a fight broke out. The crowd erupted into violence, with chairs and barricades being thrown and attenders punching each other, as others screamed and ran for safety.

Interviewed by the Guardian after the riot, Kubba claimed he had been told to start a fight with Robinson, although he declined to say by whom. Before the fight began, Robinson had said he would be willing to “batter” Kubba if the Muslim personality started a fight with him, although he also said that he liked Kubba and bore no ill will towards him. There is no suggestion the riot was pre-planned.

Over the next 20 minutes violent scenes unfolded, as Sullivan pleaded unsuccessfully with the crowd to stay peaceful. The riot was eventually broken up by event and venue security and the gardaí, who had been called to the venue earlier in the night after disturbances.

A Garda Síochána spokesperson confirmed that a male in his 30s was assaulted and removed from the scene to St Luke’s hospital in Kilkenny, while another male in his 20s was arrested and detained at a police station. A teenage boy was also arrested and subsequently charged with violent disorder, and was due to appear in court on Sunday.

Footage of the riot spread on social media in real time. Before the live stream cut to black, some in the crowd could be heard chanting “Oh Tommy Robinson”. After police dispersed the crowds, Sullivan attempted to restart the fights for the live stream only, but was blocked from doing so by a visibly upset representative of the venue.

The Ministry of Justice and the Metropolitan police were unable to confirm whether Robinson’s bail terms prohibited international travel.

In comments uploaded to social media on Sunday, Sullivan acknowledged that he had erred by not hiring more event security. “Everyone makes mistakes when you are trying to grow something,” he said, before adding: “One thing I can say is it was a great night of entertainment and it’s gone super viral.” On its official Instagram account, @unhinged_promotions, footage of the riot was uploaded with the caption: “The Area started a RIOT.”

Outside the venue in the early hours of Sunday morning, the crowd, predominantly teenage boys and young men, filmed the aftermath on their phones. One attender told the Guardian that such things did not happen in Kilkenny city, which has a population of 27,000.

#199. Nvidia will investigate CEO Jensen Huang’s relationship with possible Chinese spy

Nvidia’s board of directors announced Sunday that it’s launching an independent investigation following reports that CEO Jensen Huang was targeted by a Chinese spy operation.

Other companies involved in the AI boom have warned employees to be wary of possible spies, including those offering sex or romance.

#198. 'Never Again!': Holocaust remembrance under pressure

Only a few Holocaust survivors remain who can share their stories firsthand. Meanwhile, voices calling for an end to what they describe as a "cult of guilt" are growing louder. How can Holocaust remembrance survive?

Young people pose inside a crematorium oven at the Buchenwald concentration camp memorial, laughing while they give the Nazi salute and take pictures of each other. "In a sense, they're trophies to post on social media for them to boast about — breaking the ultimate taboo," Jens-Christian Wagner, director of the Buchenwald and Mittelbau-Dora Memorials Foundation, told DW.

"Incidents like this have become more frequent in recent years. We have seen memorial trees planted in honor of concentration camp victims cut down. When something like this happens — and it happens regularly — I find it particularly despicable because, because it ultimately kills these people a second time," added Wagner.

Wagner is part of the curatorial team behind the exhibition "Never Again! Against Forgetting the Crimes of the Nazi Regime" in Bonn's Bundeskunsthalle, or the Art and Exhibition Hall of the Federal Republic of Germany. Wagner said Holocaust remembrance is under pressure, and the subject of intensifying attacks.

Critics have dismissed remembrance as a "cult of guilt" or "national masochism." With right-wing populism on the rise and historical revisionism becoming more common, Wagner worries about how Germany will remember the victims murdered by the Nazi regime. He is also concerned about the future of democracy. And soon, there will be no Holocaust survivors left to tell the true story of what happened.

This new exhibition tackles this concern head-on with numerous works of art and historical artifacts.

"I hope that perhaps even some people who don't already believe it's important to confront Nazi crimes will find their way to the exhibition," Wagner said. Memorial sites also attract visitors who aren't necessarily convinced of the need to confront these parts of history. Many of them are students.

"Let's not kid ourselves: When a teacher says 'We're going there,' it's always a mandatory visit," he said.

For today's young people, the Nazi era seems like the distant past, Wagner explained. "When you tell them to remember something that their grandparents didn't even experience, it naturally feels like an emotional burden. They can practically see the finger being wagged at them, and that doesn't exactly encourage learning."

A two-hour lecture won't have much of an impact if pupils aren't even paying attention. Instead, Buchenwald organizes full-day programs that allow students to explore historical sources together and make sense of history for themselves.

The next step, Wagner said, is to show young people that this chapter of history isn't closed. Visitors are encouraged to make connections between the past and current events like migration, for example.

"For the longest time, people have been discussing migration almost exclusively in terms of threats to public safety and crime. This broadly marginalizes certain groups — a mechanism that also worked for National Socialism," he said.

Eva Umlauf, 83, knows firsthand what it means to experience exclusion in its most extreme form. She is a patron of the Bonn exhibition.

In November 1944, Umlauf was just 2 years old when she, her pregnant mother and her father were deported by the Nazis from the Novaky labor camp in Slovakia to Auschwitz-Birkenau, the notorious Nazi concentration and extermination camp. She was saved from the gas chambers by mere days; the Nazis stopped using the chambers as the Red Army advanced, hoping to hide evidence of their crimes.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The camp was liberated on January 27, 1945. Three months later, Umlauf's sister Nora was born. But her father, her mother's siblings and her grandparents were all murdered. Eva barely survived herself. She suffered from tuberculosis, rickets and whooping cough, as well as edema caused by starvation. "Forget about the child. She won't survive," a doctor told her mother. But he was wrong.

Her mother returned to what was then Czechoslovakia with her two daughters, settling in Trencin. Eva Umlauf has no conscious memories of Auschwitz, but she carries her family's trauma deep within her.

Her mother never talked about what happened. Even so, Eva knew from an early age that she was a survivor. Her family rarely mentioned the camp by name, but one sentence came up again and again: "They stayed there." Eva knew that meant "they" were dead. And then, there was the number tattooed onto her arm: A26959.

Her mother taught her daughters not to draw attention to their Jewish identity — antisemitism was widespread throughout Czechoslovakia at the time. The small family managed to avoid harassment.

Eva Umlauf wanted to become a pediatrician, so she studied medicine. In the 1960s, she met her future husband, Jakob, who had also survived the Holocaust. He was an architect, active in Munich's Jewish community. They married in 1966, and she moved to Germany. She could hardly have imagined that she would one day live in the country of the perpetrators. "I don't hate Germans," she said. "But I cannot forgive. How can you forgive the deaths of 6 million people?"

It wasn't until much later that she first spoke publicly about the horrors of her early childhood. In 2011, she delivered a commemorative speech in Auschwitz on the anniversary of the camp's liberation. Since 2025, she has served as chair of the International Auschwitz Committee. A heart attack in 2014 motivated her to write her first book. "I was in intensive care. I thought to myself: If I get out of here healthy, I'll write a book."

Her memoir, "The Number on Your Forearm is Blue Like Your Eyes," was first released in 2016. The English translation followed in 2024. Since its publication, hardly a day has passed without someone asking Eva Umlauf to share her story.

She is appalled by the disrespectful behavior of young people at former concentration camps and by growing ignorance about the Holocaust. Sometimes, people even ask if she's too old to have a tattoo. But she also recalls a young man on the Munich subway who noticed the number on her arm and told her: "I want to apologize to you for what my ancestors did to you."

Umlauf never imagined that antisemitism would once again become so widespread in Europe and the United States. Nor did she expect the rise of Germany's far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party.

"I go into schools. I'm so overwhelmed by the number of requests asking for my availability. And yet, the AfD is growing faster than I can reach students. It makes me think all this work is for nothing," she told DW.

Germany must never again fall under a right-wing dictatorship, she stressed. "'Never again' sounds nice, but all I can say is: It's happening again! Wouldn't 'Here We Go Again: Never Again' make a good title for the exhibition?"

She also shared her message to young people in the exhibition catalog, "Never Again": "You are not responsible for the crimes of your grandparents and great-grandparents. But you are responsible for the future. […] You are the generation that will shape our world. Make it a place where everyone can grow up safely, regardless of their background, faith or skin color."

She continues: "You don't have to be perfect. You just have to stay alert. Take care of your country."

The exhibition "Never Again! Against Forgetting the Crimes of the Nazi Regime" in the Bundeskunsthalle in Bonn runs through May 2, 2027.

This article was originally written in German.

#196. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#195. Apps to distract you from the endless cycle of doomscrolling

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

You open your phone to check the time or a quick message. The next thing you know, an hour has passed and you’ve scrolled through endless celebrity drama, cat videos, awful news stories, influencer rants, and whatever else the algorithm decided to throw at you.

Even though you probably don’t want to keep wasting your time and energy on this mind-numbing content, you do it again the next day.

Doomscrolling, the habit of spending excessive amounts of time consuming content on social media, has become incredibly widespread. A survey from last year found that 64% of Americans say they doomscroll.

Researchers have warned that doomscrolling can negatively affect several aspects of your well-being, including your mental health and attention span. Spending long periods scrolling can lead to brain fatigue, difficulty focusing, and disrupted sleep. And if a lot of the content you’re consuming is negative or stressful, it can leave you feeling disheartened, anxious, and emotionally drained.

It’s hard to break the cycle of doomscrolling, but there are plenty of apps that provide content that’s engaging and productive.

Of course, you could always read a book or go for a walk (we have a guide on how to stop doomscrolling), but this list is for when you have a few spare minutes and want something to do on your phone that isn’t endless scrolling.

Finch is a self-care pet app that’s designed to help you build healthy habits. You care for a cute virtual pet by completing small daily goals, such as drinking enough water, going on a walk, and practicing mindfulness. You can write about your day, complete a breathing exercise, or send good vibes to a friend by adding them to the app.

The more self-care you do, the more your pet thrives. The app offers a fun way to trade mindless scrolling for small moments of self-care.

Finch is available for free on iOS and Android.

Libby is a free reading app that connects you to your local public library’s digital collection of ebooks, audiobooks, and magazines. All you need is a library card to borrow thousands of titles, which makes it easy to turn screen time into something productive.

You can explore a new book during a commute or while waiting at a doctor’s office, turning moments when your fingers might otherwise itch to scroll into opportunities to read.

Libby is available for free on iOS and Android.

If you want to take a break from doomscrolling and try something creative, Dudel Draw might be a good app for you. Each day, the app gives you a random shape that you turn into a drawing. These daily shapes vary from basic geometrical forms to more complex and abstract designs.

You can also get your friends in on the creativity with some fun competition by comparing your different creations, which could provide a nice break from sending each other endless TikToks or Instagram Reels.

If you still want to feel connected to the world without scrolling through social media, you can check out Radio Garden. The app lets you listen to over 25,000 live radio stations from across the globe.

Once you open the app, you’ll see green dots marking cities and towns. Tap any dot to listen to radio stations broadcasting from that location. You can add favorites or search for radio stations, countries, and places.

Radio Garden is free, but it also offers an ad-free premium plan for $2.99 per month. The app is available on both iOS and Android.

Elevate is an app designed to help improve focus, memory, reading, math skills, and other cognitive abilities. It includes over 40 games to train different abilities you use in everyday life, from reading faster to comparing prices more efficiently.

You can track training streaks and compare your mind’s performance over time.

The app offers a free version with access to three games per day, or a yearly subscription of $39.99 for unlimited access. It’s available on both iOS and Android.

Vocabulary is an app that helps you learn new words every day. You can pick your difficulty level and choose categories that interest you, like emotions, the human body, business, and more. Each word comes with a definition, example sentences, and a guide on how to pronounce it.

The app also includes mini-games to help you review the words you’ve learned. You can set a goal of how many words you want to learn each week and create a regular learning routine.

Vocabulary offers a free trial, and then costs $4.99 per month or $59.99 per year. It’s available on iOS and Android.

If you’re a geography nerd looking to test your knowledge or just want to improve your geography skills, Seterra is perfect for you. The app features over 300 different games to test your map skills. You can test your knowledge of world flags; discover oceans, seas, and rivers; and explore mountain ranges and volcanoes across the globe.

Seterra lets you track your progress across categories and see leaderboards for top scorers for each game.

The app is free and available on both iOS and Android.

The NYT Games app offers several word, logic, and number games that change every day to exercise your mind. You can play the crossword, try the word-guessing game Wordle, group words with a common theme in Connections, see how many words you can make from seven letters in Spelling Bee, and more.

The app costs $5.99 per month for unlimited access and archives, but some games like Wordle, Strands, and the Mini Crossword are available to play for free.

NYT Games is available on both iOS and Android.

If you want to learn a new language but want to try something other than Duolingo, Drops is a good option. The app uses visually engaging mini‑games to help you build vocabulary and common phrases in more than 45 languages, with bite‑sized lessons designed to be completed in about five minutes a day.

Drops is designed for both beginners and experts who want to grow their foreign language vocabulary.

The free version of the app offers five-minute lessons per day. You can get unlimited access and premium features for $11.99 per month or $79.99 per year. The app is available on both iOS and Android.

This story was originally published in March 2026 and is updated regularly with new information.

#194. Gaza: Rains add misery to humanitarian catastrophe

With most homes damaged or destroyed by the war, Gazans are facing worsening humanitarian conditions due to heavy rain.

Heavy rains sweeping across parts of the Middle East this week have flooded tens of thousands of tents in Gaza's displaced persons' camps.

According to authorities in the enclave, at least 70,000 tents have been flooded over the past day, where tens of thousands of people took shelter.

No fatalities had been reported, although some people were injured.

Some of the tents have been washed away due to the weather, with mattresses and blankets soaked with water.

More than 1.6 million residents of Gaza's 2.1-million population now live in tents or other makeshift shelters due to Israel's war in the enclave.

UN data says some 80% of the enclave's structures have been damaged during the war, with two-thirds destroyed, leaving Gaza's residents — many of whom were displaced multiple times during the war between Israel and Hamas — to either live in tents or risk their lives and seek shelter in buildings that could be on the verge of collapse.

The Higher Presidential Committee for Church Affairs in Palestine released a statement appealing for humanitarian aid for the Gaza Strip due to the situation.

According to the committee, some 950,000 people are living in areas "vulnerable to flooding," with more than 400,000 living in areas of "high levels of risk."

"Heavy rainfall is compounding an already catastrophic humanitarian situation, leaving displaced families and those who have lost their homes exposed to the elements and deprived of the most basic necessities of life," the statement said.

Some 74,000 people have been killed in the Gaza Strip since October 7, 2023. The war was triggered when Islamist Palestinian organization Hamas and its affiliates launched a terrorist attack in Israel's south, killing some 1,200 Israelis, most of whom were civilians. The militants also kidnapped 251 hostages.

The UN, EU, US and Israel, among others, consider Hamas to be a terrorist organization.

A UN commission of inquiry, as well as many leading human rights organizations, have accused Israel of committing genocide in Gaza. Israel has rejected the accusation, saying its army is acting in accordance with international law.

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

#190. Russell Brand case must be wake-up call to protect women in media, Lisa Nandy says

Culture secretary says there is still a problem with exploitation and abuse in the industry and public life

The culture secretary, Lisa Nandy, has said there is still a problem with exploitation and abuse in the media industry and a failure to properly deal with allegations after Russell Brand’s conviction for rape and sexual assault.

She said women who came forward were too often not protected by institutions across public life, including in politics, and that huge power imbalances meant speaking up came at a very heavy cost.

Nandy told the media industry that “time is up” to bring in systems to protect vulnerable whistleblowers, and said that while the BBC had taken action in recent years, other organisations still had to take the problem seriously.

Brand’s conviction on Thursday renewed scrutiny of how the UK’s entertainment industry has treated celebrities accused of abusing their power, with some women saying they feared that speaking out could harm their careers.

Brand faces sentencing on Monday after being found guilty of raping a woman and sexually assaulting three others. The judge said he faces a “significant” prison term.

Nandy said last week that the case must be a wake-up call to the media world and beyond, after widespread concerns over failures to confront Brand’s behaviour. It emerged after the verdicts that he is under investigation for two further rape allegations.

In an interview with the BBC’s Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg programme, Nandy was asked if she thought there was still a problem in the media and creative industries.

“Yes, I do, and I think, hand on heart, I can’t say to you as the culture secretary that this is not happening today. In fact, if I had to wager, I would say it is,” she said.

“The biggest reason I say that, and I won’t say it lightly, is because I do not see that much has changed in terms of the way that institutions in the media industry and across public life, including politics, deal with women who very bravely come forward to allege abuse. I think they’re not protected.

“I think that they work in industries like politics and media where the power imbalances are huge, where they’re often on short-term flexible contracts, and where who you know and reputation is often the key to career advancement. Speaking up has a very heavy cost.”

She praised the BBC for signing up to the Creative Industries Independent Standards Authority (CIISA), a whistleblowing body, and for taking other steps in recent years, but said there was more the corporation could do.

“There are other institutions that haven’t done that, and time is up,” she said. “It really is time for the media industry to take this seriously and to put those practices into action.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Nandy had said earlier on Sky News Sunday Morning that the government had a responsibility to make sure Brand’s conviction was a “watershed moment” and was considering what further steps it could take to help protect whistleblowers in such circumstances.

“I would urge every organisation that hasn’t already signed up to CIISA … to do so. But I would also say to them that if they don’t, we are considering what further steps we can take as a government to mandate that sort of action.”

Brand’s case has led to difficult questions for the BBC, Channel 4 and the production companies that worked with him. The TV world is again asking itself if recent efforts to improve safeguarding will stop similar abuses.

The comedian and presenter also wrote columns for the Guardian and the New Statesman and appeared before a parliamentary select committee to share his views on drugs policies.

A BBC editorial standards report in January last year found that staff felt unable to complain about Brand because of the culture at Radio 2 and Radio 6 Music, where he presented shows.

Allegations included claims that he exposed himself in the studio, urinated into bottles, threw objects in anger and – according to his own memoir – had sex with a competition winner in a disabled toilet. The BBC formally apologised to those affected.

#189. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

#186. GPs to offer 300,000 patients extra support to ease winter strain on NHS

£40m scheme in England aims to keep most vulnerable people well at home and avoid unnecessary hospital stays

Hundreds of thousands of vulnerable patients in England are to receive extra support from their GP as part of a £40m drive aimed at keeping them out of hospital this winter.

As the NHS gears up for the pressures of the coming months, family doctors will offer 300,000 people with frailty and long-term conditions health checks, medication reviews and respiratory tests designed to prevent avoidable hospital admissions.

Funded by cash reallocated from existing GP budgets for 2026-27, the scheme will allow surgeries to identify and provide extra care to the patients they believe are most at risk of becoming seriously unwell in the winter. Groups of GP practices can use £126 for every eligible patient supported.

The prime minister said the scheme would be targeted at patients with heart disease, frailty and lung conditions such as emphysema and other types of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD).

Andy Burnham said he was “not going to pretend this winter will be easy” but the programme would help keep people well at home and avoid unnecessary trips to hospital.

“If we want to take the pressure off our hospitals this winter, we’ve got to keep people well at home. So we’re backing GPs to reach the most vulnerable people early, before a small problem turns into a trip to A&E,” he said.

“People deserve the right care in the right place. And our NHS staff, who work so hard, deserve the right tools to provide it. I’m not going to pretend this winter will be easy. But with new technology, upgrades for hospitals and more care available closer to home, we’ll make sure the NHS is there for everyone when they need it.”

The health secretary, Yvette Cooper, said the scheme had been designed to offer greater support to the frailest patients.

“After such a heavy summer for our NHS as a result of the heat, everyone is working together to prepare for more challenging winter pressures,” she said. “That includes the rollout of flu vaccinations, investing in extra urgent care capacity and strengthening partnerships with social care.

“With thousands more GPs in post and millions more GP appointments this year, the £40m programme to support vulnerable patients in the community is part of our plan to help patients, improve primary care and also to support our NHS through the heavier winter months.”

Prof Victoria Tzortziou Brown, the president of the Royal College of GPs, said she welcomed the focus on prevention and supporting patients at greatest risk of avoidable hospital admission this winter.

“GPs’ clinical expertise, knowledge of their patients and continuity of care are central to identifying those most in need and providing personalised, coordinated support,” she said.

But she also said short-term initiatives alone could not deliver “sustained improvements in care” that patients with complex health needs required. Instead, she said, a longer-term approach to prevention was necessary, which must be backed by “sustainable investment in general practice”.

The US military said a vessel trying to leave an Iranian port was hit due to the naval blockade on the country. On its part, Iran said three "non compliant" oil tankers caught fire in the Strait of Hormuz.

The United States struck a commercial ship attempting to breach its naval blockade on Iran, the US military said Sunday.

According to the US Central Command (CENTCOM), the cargo vessel "tried to depart regional waters after visiting an Iranian port," before its stern was struck by "precision munitions" shot from a US military aircraft.

In a video posted by CENTCOM, an explosion can be seen on the M/V Huda's stern as a result of the shooting, with the ship's civilian crew ignoring "repeated warnings."

The authorities in the UAE said 22 people had been rescued due to a fire on a vessel in the Gulf of Oman.

The US naval blockade on Iran, intended to pressure the country into lifting its own blockade on the Strait of Hormuz, has been in force for months.

Iran's own blockade of the waterway, through which a fifth of the world's oil and gas supplies flowed before the war started in late February, saw energy prices spike around the world.

While Houthis carried out such attacks in the past, the rebels have not claimed responsibility for the attack yet.

According to the Houthis, 33 people were killed in Saudi-led attacks on Yemen's rebel-held capital, Sanaa, on Saturday.

#183. Teaching GCSE topics younger risks turning schools into ‘early exam factories’, Powell warns

Lucy Powell says proposals to end widespread practice will be included in overhaul of England’s national curriculum

Pushing GCSE subjects on younger pupils risks turning England’s secondary schools into “early exam factories”, the education secretary has said, warning that it is increasing disengagement from education.

Lucy Powell wants schools to end the widespread practice of teaching GCSE topics to pupils in year 9 or earlier, before they officially start in year 10, and says she plans to reduce the amount of GCSE content to make it easier to fit into the official timetable.

The Department for Education (DfE) said the proposals would be included in the government’s overhaul of the national curriculum to be published in early 2027, as part of its plans to tackle school disengagement as well as the disadvantage gap between pupils from rich and poor backgrounds and the rising number of young people not in education, employment or training.

Powell said: “The early years of secondary school should be a time when children are excited to go to school and engage in the specialist and much broader teaching on offer, and where they find their passion and develop a love of learning.

“Instead, we’ve seen worrying trends of disengagement and high levels of persistent absence, with the disadvantage gap stubbornly large and too many leaving school without the knowledge and skills they need to thrive.

“That’s what our exciting and engaging new national curriculum aims to address as part of our wider education reforms.

“What’s more, the pressures of GCSEs and an overcrowded curriculum are resulting in too many children receiving a narrower offer … and being rushed towards exams without grasping the fundamentals or developing deeper understanding. I’m putting a stop to that.”

Many secondary schools start introducing GCSE-level material to pupils in year 9 as a way of spreading out the course content. But Powell told the Sunday Times she was opposed to “early exam factories” and that plans to reduce GCSEs’ content would enable the courses to be taught comfortably within the allotted two years of year 10 and year 11.

The new curriculum will say schools “should not start GCSE specifications early for large cohorts or see children routinely required to narrow their options earlier or entered earlier for exams”, the DfE stated.

However, the new policy will not be compulsory, and schools will retain the flexibility to teach GCSEs to younger students whom they judge to be capable of coping with it.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Powell said slimmed-down GCSEs would give schools “more time to develop deeper understanding, as well as a richer experience that will engage more young people in their passions and prepare them better for the fast-changing world they will grow up in”.

Powell also said she supported new efforts to help the government and other groups improve educational outcomes for children from white working-class communities.

“A child’s background should never mean opportunity belongs to someone else,” she said.

“But right now, too many white working-class children are falling behind at every stage of their education.”

#181. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.
