#148. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#146. US midterm results may help unite Israeli coalition to unseat Netanyahu

Democrats’ sweep could urge Israel’s divided opposition to join forces and remove prime minister closely allied with Trump

The Democrats’ sweep of Congress may make it easier for Israel’s splintered opposition to overcome deep internal divisions and form a coalition government, by increasing the urgency of unseating Benjamin Netanyahu, who has associated himself so closely with Donald Trump’s Republicans.

But as Israel took stock of the results, there was also a sense that the elections marked a longer-term shift in US politics that will make the job of any new Israeli leader far more difficult.

The United States provides Israel with critical military and diplomatic support, so nurturing bipartisan ties was the cornerstone of foreign policy for decades. Netanyahu ripped up that commitment to throw his lot in with the Republicans in general and Trump in particular.

The opposition had always attacked his decision as ill-judged. By the end of the week, they were warning that it was positively dangerous.

“These people aren’t against Israel,” prominent opposition politician Yair Lapid said of incoming Democratic lawmakers, urging his country to rebuild bridges. “They’re mainly against Netanyahu – for them, anything connected to him is toxic.”

“The easiest thing is to scream that ‘America is lost,’ because if it’s lost, you don’t have to work. That’s just not true. Not at all.”

Lapid laid out a plan to “rebuild trust” in the US, but it perhaps did more to demonstrate the scale of the problem than reassure Israelis.

His proposal of a transatlantic charm offensive by officials of a prospective new government drew a swift and sharp rebuke from the New York congressman-elect Brad Lander.

Lander, a Democrat who is Jewish and describes himself as a liberal Zionist, was detained in the occupied West Bank last month while joining Palestinians trying to harvest olives.

“Minister Lapid: As someone who won a seat in Congress last night, and who cares about Israel: you’re not listening to what American voters – Jewish and non-Jewish – are saying,” Lander wrote in a direct response.

“We aren’t starving for a barnstorming tour. We’re hungry to see respect for international law and human rights.”

In his exchange with Lapid, Lander also highlighted the arrest of journalist Abed Abu Shehada, a Palestinian citizen of Israel, as an example of how Israel violates international law. He drew a direct link with the ICE “regime of detention without charges, based on secret evidence, designed to intimidate and chill speech” that he said was rejected by US voters.

Abu Shehada was reportedly taken from his home by 15 masked security officials in a night-time raid, without an arrest warrant. Interrogators have only questioned him about a podcast interview with a public figure, lawyers acting for Abu Shehada said.

On Friday, Lander and two other incoming Jewish members of Congress, Daniel Bliss and Scott Wiener, wrote to Israel’s ambassador to the US calling for the immediate release of Abu Shehada.

“Detaining a reporter for conducting an interview — the only issue that interrogators questioned him about — is a violation of international law,” Lander said when he published the letter. “It comes at a time when senior Israeli officials are trying to delegitimize the participation of Palestinian citizens in Israel’s recent elections.”

Nor do Israel’s problems end with angry Democrats. The wounded Republican establishment lost no time looking a few thousand miles away to find reasons for the collapse of its voter base.

Soon after the election results came in, the vice-president, JD Vance, put “our relationship with Israel” on a short list of reasons for voter fury, and on election day Trump made a last-ditch attempt to woo voters with a pledge to cut off $4bn (£3bn) in annual military aid to Israel.

The announcement caused panic in Israel’s defence establishment. “When the commander of the Israel air force hears something like that, he might slit his wrists,” one official told Channel 12 news. “Where are we going to come up with 15 billion shekels every year, and what does that mean for Israel’s future defence budget?”

Netanyahu only responded to Trump’s electioneering on Friday with an attempt to spin the cut as a joint decision reflecting Israel’s economic strength. It demonstrated a “move from aid to partnership”, his office said in a statement.

Netanyahu has long flaunted his relationship with Trump, his fluent English and experience in the US, presenting himself as the only man with the international standing to guarantee Israel’s security in a hostile world.

After Tuesday’s US election, he looks more like an agent of its destruction to many in Israel. “One thing is clear: we are losing America,” Ben Caspit wrote in Ma’ariv, a daily newspaper.

“This isn’t a process; it isn’t happening in stages; it is happening all at once. It is an avalanche. It is a reverberating collapse of the foundation of Israel’s national defence doctrine. This isn’t a decree of fate. It is a decree of Netanyahu.”

Some advocates for change inside Israel found cause for hope in the Democrats’ victory, if Israel’s opposition can turn their mandate for change into a new government. They won 67 seats in the 120-seat Knesset, but more than a quarter went to Palestinian citizens of Israel, and some of the Jewish Israeli opposition parties have ruled out sitting with them.

US administrations have for too long equated support for Israel with blind support for its government, said Nadav Tamir, a former senior Israeli diplomat in the US who is now executive director of J Street Israel.

That consensus is cracking, but the majority of lawmakers from both parties still see Israel as a key US ally in the Middle East, he wrote in the Times of Israel.

External pressure from those who support its security but see current policy as self-destructive might push the country towards necessary change, including a resolution of the Israel-Palestine conflict.

“Perhaps this will be the paradox of the new [US] Congress,” he wrote. “It will be less willing to define support for Israel as automatic alignment with every policy of its government, but precisely for that reason, it may offer Israel more meaningful support in breaking out of the cycle of wars and building a future of security and prosperity.”

#144. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#141. Germany: Chancellor Merz to nominate Aigner for president

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz announced that he will nominate Ilse Aigner for federal president, paving the way for her to be the first woman to hold the office. DW has more.

These updates are now closed. Here is a roundup of the top stories from and about Germany on the weekend of Saturday, November 7, and Sunday, November 8, 2026:

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz announced to the Christian Democrat Union (CDU) party heads his intention to nominate Ilse Aigner as the country's next federal president, according to the dpa, AFP and Reuters news agencies.

She will replace Frank-Walter Steinmeier when he steps down at the end of his term early next year.

Aigner, who hails from Bavaria's conservative Christian Social Union (CSU) and is the president of the Bavarian state parliament, would be the first woman to hold the German president's position should she be elected on January 30.

Steinmeier is a member of the junior coalition partner SPD.

Merz will officially pitch Aigner's name for the job on Monday, when party leaders will gather to choose a joint candidate.

SPD and Green Party representatives have already voiced support for her candidacy.

The city of Moscow has officially unveiled a new commemorative plaque honoring one of communist East Germany's top Cold War spies, Markus Wolf.

The plaque was initially installed by Russia's Foreign Intelligence Service (SVR) at Nizhny Kislovsky Lane 8 in September to mark the site where Wolf and his family, who were religious Jews and communists, lived between 1934 and 1945 after fleeing Nazi Germany.

Wolf would go on to spend nearly 30 years at the top of the foreign department of East Germany's Ministry for State Security, or Stasi, and was considered one of the Cold War's ultimate spymasters.

The spymaster was long known as "the man without a face" by Western intelligence services because they had no idea what he looked like.

Wolf's ability to place agent Günter Guillaume within West German Chancellor Willy Brandt's inner circle, ultimately leading to Brandt's 1974 resignation, is considered to be one of his greatest successes.

Wolf's widow Andrea reportedly expressed her gratitude for the honor in a letter addressed to "all Russian friends." Wolf died in Berlin on November 9, 2006, at the age of 83.

The Berlin Police and the Berlin Public Prosecutor's Office on Sunday evening issued a joint statement announcing that they believe that a body found in an abandoned house in Brandenburg is in fact that of Lisa T., a 13-year-old Berlin girl who went missing on her way to school Tuesday morning.

Police in Brandenburg found the body, as well as an injured male whom they believe to be responsible for the child's death, when they acted on a tip from locals alerting them of a break-in.

Authorities say the man is in critical condition at a nearby hospital where he is under police guard. Further, authorities say the man's injuries were self-induced.

Police are now working to ascertain the man's identity.

On Friday, the police said that the girl had likely not disappeared voluntarily. On Saturday, they said that it must be assumed that she had been the victim of a crime.

Veteran keeper Manuel Neuer has been added to the list of walking wounded at Bundesliga top team FC Bayern Munich, suffering a torn foot tendon in his team's 2-1 victory in Mainz.

Neuer, who posted on social media that "an injury never comes at the right time, but it's part of the game," left the match just before the half.

Bayern released a statement saying that the champion goalie would be "out for the time being."

Though no further information was given, media reports suggested Neuer could be out for at least two months.

"I've been through this before, and I'll get through this one, too," said Neuer. "From now on: full focus on recovery. Until then, I will support the boys on the pitch wherever I can."

A two-time Champions League winner, Neuer made club history at the start of the match — his 550th in Germany's Bundesliga — as the oldest ever player to take the pitch for Bayern at age 40 years and 225 days.

The veteran's absence will only compound Bayern's injury woes as they battle on without head coach Vincent Kompany — who is recovering from an illness — as well as Jamal Musiala, Luis Diaz, Ismael Saibari and Serge Gnabry — all of whom are on long-term injured reserve.

As authorities in Poland try to make sense of yesterday's deadly shooting in Krakow, lists of victims' nationalities have been made public.

Of the six victims shot and killed at the city's market square on Saturday, four were women and two were men. Three of the women and one of the men were Polish, while the remaining woman was from Austria and the man from the UK.

Austrian, British, German, Portuguese and Russian passport holders were among the 15 individuals wounded by the Polish gunman.

German Federal President Frank-Walter Steinmeier's office on Sunday posted a statement of support on X, writing: "In this difficult hour, Germany stands in mourning and solidarity with Poland and its citizens."

Further, Steinmeier expressed his condolences to Polish President Karol Nawocki, saying, "my thoughts are with the victims and their families."

Researchers have confirmed the loss of the Schwarzmilzferner, the last glacier in the Allgäu Alps on the border between Germany and Austria.

Estimates by researchers say that the eastern Alps could be completely ice-free by 2070 due to climate change and rising global temperatures.

The glacier was located on the Austrian side of the mountain range at an altitude of 2,450 meters (8038 feet).

It can no longer be considered a glacier as it has lost over 4 meters in thickness and around half its volume earlier this year, glaciologist Cristoph Mayer told the dpa news agency.

Mayer said that the glacier will likely disappear "completely" by next summer.

The rapid melting came as Europe was hit by a massive heatwave over the summer of 2026.

"Even if there is heavy snowfall in winter, the ice masses are shrinking rapidly. Summers have now become too hot for them," Mayer said.

"As things stand, I expect the entire Eastern Alps to be ice-free between 2060 and 2070."

A 22-year-old man took down the German flag at the Reichstag building in Berlin and replaced it with a Palestinian flag, police said on Sunday.

The Reichstag building is the seat of the lower house of German parliament, the Bundestag. The large German flag that flies in front of the building is known as the Unity Flag, or Flagge der Einheit in German, as it commemorates the German reunification of 1990.

The man climbed up the flagpole using a climbing harness at around 11:30 a.m. local time (1030 UTC) and threw the German flag to the ground, police said.

He lowered himself back down on his own, according to police, after which he was taken into custody.

He was supported by two other men aged 20 and 32, according to the report.

Police have launched an investigation into a suspected case of denigration of the state and its symbols, damaging public property and trespassing.

The Palestinian flag was removed with the assistance of the fire department.

As the mast hoisting cable had been damaged in the incident, the German flag could not be immediately raised again.

A camel survived a collision with a bus in southern Germany.

The accident occurred on a rural road in Riedering, a town southeast of Munich in the state of Bavaria.

According to police, three camels escaped a fenced pasture, with one of them ending up on the road on which the accident occurred when the bus drove past it at 8am local time.

While the camel did not sustain any injuries, the bus suffered an estimated €500 ($552) worth of damage.

Bavarian Premier Markus Söder has spoken out in favor of military conscription in remarks seemingly in response to Defense Minister Boris Pistorius, who this week said he believed the draft was not necessary in the near future (see earlier entry in this blog).

"If our democracy is important to us, we have to stand up for it. That's why it is clear: We need true military conscription again," he wrote on X.

"Germany is threatened in a way it has not been in a long time," Söder wrote. "That is why we have to keep strengthening the Bundeswehr."

Söder, who also leads the conservative Christian Social Union (CSU), which forms part of Germany's coalition government, said the current large investments in equipment and infrastructure were not enough on their own.

"A new concept of defense for the country and its allies also needs more soldiers," he wrote.

In remarks published on Saturday, Pistorius told newspapers from the Funke media group that the Bundeswehr currently had some 190,000 members, the highest level in 13 years, meaning that voluntary recruitment measures were working.

"If this trajectory continues, we will have no problems with growth," he said, and there would not have to be "a discussion about conscription of whatever kind."

During Gen Z protests last weekend, young demonstrators called among other things for an end to any considerations of reintroducing military conscription.

Germany's largest union, IG Metall, has called on workers at a Mercedes-Benz plant outside Berlin to walk out for two hours on Monday amid stalled wage negotiations.

Employees at local car supplier Gestamp in Ludwigsfelde, a town south of Berlin, have also been called upon to take part in the industrial action.

IG Metall is demanding 5% more pay for the around 96,000 employees in the metal and electrical industry in the states of Berlin and Brandenburg as part of a 12-month collective wage agreement, as well as job and location security.

The strike is being called after the union failed to reach agreement in a third round of a wage dispute.

During a second round of talks, the employers presented a coordinated nationwide offer of a one-off payment next year and a 1.3% pay rise in July 2028, which was rejected by the union as inadequate.

Young people are increasingly denying the facts of the Holocaust or downplaying its horrors, according to the head of the Anne Frank Zentrum in Berlin.

In remarks made to the Catholic news agency KNA ahead of the 88th anniversary of the November pogroms in Germany, Veronika Nahm said that people attending traveling exhibitions organized by the center sometimes asked, "Is that even true?"

She said that people downplaying or denying the Holocaust was particularly apparent in social media posts connected with the center.

She said there were comments such as, "Why do you commemorate Anne Frank, why not a child in Gaza?"

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Nahm attributes the rise in such opinions to ignorance rather than bad intentions on the part of young people, saying they were not as well-informed on the topic in schools as they were 15 years ago.

"There has been a generational change of teachers. For many older teachers, it was close to their hearts, and it was mentioned in many places and subjects during lessons. That is different today," she said.

Nahm said it was important that visits by young people to sites commemorating the Holocaust be used to strengthen their feeling of empathy, something that worked particularly well with the story of Anne Frank.

The Anne Frank Zentrum in Berlin is a partner organization to the Anne Frank House in Amsterdam.

Anne Frank was a Jewish girl who hid in that house during the 1940-1945 German occupation of the Netherlands and wrote a famous diary, later published, about her experiences and feelings.

She was discovered and deported by the Nazis and died in the Bergen-Belsen concentration camp in early 1945.

The November pogroms, during which Jews and Jewish property and institutions were attacked, took place on November 9-10, 1938, across the German Reich at the instigation of Germany's Nazi regime.

Most people in Germany favor the government imposing a cap on prices for gasoline and diesel, a survey has shown.

The government is currently debating such a cap, which would replace a current fuel tax rebate that runs out at the end of the year.

According to the survey by the NIM Institute, 71% of the people asked said they would like the government to control fuel prices.

Altogether 66% of respondents were in favor of levying a windfall tax on oil corporations, which have been turning in massive profits amid shortages caused largely by the war in Iran.

Other measures to relieve the pressure on motorists amid surging fuel prices were less popular, according to the survey.

Just 56% of respondents wanted to see a rise in the allowed tax deduction for commuters, and just 52% favored direct payments from the state to drivers on low incomes.

German Environment Minister Carsten Schneider has said he hopes agreement will be reached on measures to limit global warming at the UN Climate Change Conference over the next two weeks.

"The faster global emissions fall, the faster we can bring the temperature curve back down to 1.5 degrees Celsius (2.7 degrees Fahrenheit). At the same time, we must do more to protect people from the effects of heat waves, droughts and floods," Schneider said.

Schneider was referring to the target of limiting global warming to 1.5 C compared with pre-industrial levels agreed on under the Paris climate accords.

The UN said last month, however, that this target will be missed within the century, with most nations failing to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions by a significant amount.

Schneider also said moving toward electricity as the main source of energy was the best way to combat climate change, which is being driven largely by the human burning of fossil fuels such as oil and gas.

Schneider will be the only German Cabinet member to attend the  COP31 summit, which kicks off in Turkey on Monday.

Chancellor Friedrich Merz has decided to skip the event, citing "other commitments."

His decision has drawn considerable criticism, with leading German climate activist Luisa Neubauer saying it sent "the worst possible signal to the international community."

"It is unworthy of Germany and an insult, particularly to those nations around the world that are already facing an existential threat from rising sea levels," she said.

Media reports say Merz is planning a visit to an Italian restaurant with his wife and daughter for his 71st birthday on Wednesday while the summit is underway, leading Neubauer to describe his non-appearance as a "Bella Italia absence."

Nearly 200 states will convene for the summit in Antalya.

Police in Berlin are still looking for a 13-year-old girl who disappeared on the way to school on Tuesday.

In a statement, Berlin police said they could not rule out that the girl, who has been identified as Lisa T., "has been the victim of a crime."

"Based on the current state of the investigation, it is assumed that the child did not leave her home environment voluntarily," police said.

Around 220 police officers were deployed in the Marzahn district of the German capital on Saturday, with a police spokesman saying that the search was time-consuming because of the large number of houses and parks in the area.

The search began on Friday, supported by sniffer dogs and a police helicopter.

Women on the executive boards of Germany's leading listed companies were earning almost as much on average as their male colleagues in 2025, a study by consultancy EY says.

According to the study, female board members at companies listed on the DAX, MDAX and SDAX indices, not including chief executives, earned an average of €2.20 million ($2.45 million) last year.

Their male counterparts, on the other hand, saw a nearly 2% reduction in their pay down to €2.23 million, which reduced the pay gap from €121,000 to €26,000.

The study also found that there were more women serving on executive boards in 2025 than a year previously, with 101 female board members compared with 88 in 2024 and just 33 in 2013.

"Women on executive boards are no longer the exception. That is also reflected in salaries," said EY partner Jens Massmann.

#138. Strait of Hormuz could reopen as part of Qatari plan for Iran nuclear inspections

Trump still considering new assault on Iran nuclear sites – though threats less credible after Vance remarks

Qatar, the lead mediator in the US-Iran conflict, is seeking to persuade Tehran to allow UN nuclear weapons inspectors to visit Iran’s bombed nuclear sites, including those housing the country’s stockpile of highly enriched uranium.

Iran would not be required to make any commitment that the stockpile would be removed from the country, with other options on the table, including reducing the uranium content by down-blending it.

Sheikh Tamim bin Hamad al-Thani, the emir of Qatar, spoke to Donald Trump about the proposal, the emir’s office said on Friday without revealing the call’s contents.

The state news agency said the emir “emphasised the importance of continuing dialogue and embracing diplomacy as a means of resolving disputes, preventing further escalation in the region”.

Trump is still considering whether to mount a further assault on Iranian nuclear sites, though the credibility of his threats is weaker inside Iran after his vice-president, JD Vance, said the war itself, and not just its cost to American consumers, was critical to the collapse of the Republican vote in the US midterms.

Under the Qatar proposal, the US would immediately lift the naval blockade on Iran in the strait of Hormuz and release a portion of the $8bn (£6bn) of its frozen assets. In return, Iran would agree to visits by International Atomic Energy Agency inspectors to its bombed nuclear facilities, based on a previous abandoned agreement that had been reached in Cairo in September 2025.

Iran would open the strait of Hormuz on the second day, via a route it has already negotiated with Oman that they would jointly lodge with the International Maritime Organisation (IMO).

Iran has multiple other preconditions for opening the strait including ending the war in Lebanon, a withdrawal of US forces from the Iranian coastline, a commitment to lift some economic sanctions, and agreement to pay war reparations. The sequencing of these commitments has been at the heart of the mediation.

In the memorandum of understanding, a $300bn reconstruction and development fund was to be set up to help Iran recover economically, but it was never clear exactly how this proposal was to be funded.

Iran, in a triumphalist mood after Trump’s election wipeout, has been wary of allowing the UN nuclear inspectors back into the country, claiming it had lost faith in Rafael Grossi, the head of the inspectorate.

Tehran claims Grossi betrayed his duty to uphold the nuclear non-proliferation treaty, by failing to criticise the US for bombing Iran’s nuclear sites. It fears that material gathered by the inspectors would be handed over as targeting data to the Americans and Israelis.

In the parallel talks on opening the strait, Oman’s foreign minister, Badr Albusaidi, spoke again to Iran about the future of the strait administration including the agreed route, the extent to which the agreement is temporary and the need to register the proposal with the IMO, either as a bilateral agreement or a joint regional proposal.

Oman has a long-term proposal for the administration of the strait compliant with the law of the sea in that shipping companies would be charged only for services, and not tolls.

Amir Hayat Moqaddam, the deputy chair of the Iranian parliament’s national security and foreign policy committee, told the Iranian Students’ News Agency (ISNA) on Tuesday that ships from four countries – China, India, Qatar and Iraq – were allowed to pass through the strategic waterway.

The exemptions served mutual interests, he said, adding that Iran charged Indian and Chinese vessels transit fees while allowing Qatari ships to pass free of charge. Medicine was received from India, while China provided political support.

It is reported that of the 10.23m barrels a day of Gulf crude that passed through the strait in October, about 42% went to China and 13% to India, said Muyu Xu, a senior crude oil analyst at the analytics firm Kpler.

The navy of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) said two “violating” oil tankers attempting to pass through an unauthorised route in the eastern sector of the strait of Hormuz had collided with mines and caught fire.

The IRGC said the two tankers had turned off their navigation and identification systems and that the attempted crossing was with the support of US military forces.

#134. Court rules against Home Office in asylum seeker kidnapping case

Department had claimed that being kidnapped for ransom was not exploitation of trafficking victims in case of Syrian man abducted in Libya

The court of appeal has rejected claims by the Home Office that being kidnapped for ransom is not exploitation of trafficking victims.

The ruling means that many people who have been kidnapped for ransom, whom officials have previously excluded from being victims of exploitation under trafficking rules, will now have to have these claims considered. They may be eligible for the support and recovery package trafficking victims are entitled to.

Being kidnapped for ransom is a common hazard for asylum seekers passing through Libya on their journey to Europe. Many are fleeing conflict in countries such as Sudan and Eritrea as well as some Middle Eastern countries.

The legal challenge was brought by a Syrian asylum seeker who cannot be identified. The 25-year-old fled forced military conscription in July 2021, knowing he was likely to be imprisoned and tortured in Syria for refusing to fight.

He arrived in Libya, ultimately hoping to reach the UK. He and other Syrians were abducted by armed men and held in captivity for a month between August and September 2021. His money and phone were seized and they were driven through the desert for 24 hours, hearing gunshots, with no food and little water.

They were told they would not be released until their families agreed to pay a ransom of several thousand dollars. He was tied up and often beaten on his hands and feet by his kidnappers. He and the other captives had to phone their families to beg them to pay their kidnappers the ransom money. While they were on the phone, the kidnappers beat them so their families could hear screams. He was terrified that even if his family paid his captors, he might not be released and that his organs might be harvested.

The man was released after his family paid his kidnappers, and he made his way to Europe, crossing the Mediterranean from Libya to Italy by boat. He crossed the Channel from France to the UK on 16 May 2022 and claimed asylum.

The Home Office rejected his trafficking claim, arguing that because he had not been subjected to forced labour by his kidnappers, his experience did not meet the definition of exploitation in the trafficking rules. He challenged the decision in the high court, which ruled in his favour. The home secretary, Shabana Mahmood, appealed against the ruling.

While the government has pledged to support trafficking victims, the decision to exclude asylum seekers kidnapped for ransom in Libya has denied many the support the judges ruled they may be entitled to. Recent freedom of information data relating to asylum seekers who were confirmed to be genuine trafficking victims found that of the 1,778 confirmed victims between July 2024 and July 2025, 89% were forcibly removed from the UK, putting them at risk of being trafficked again.

While each trafficking claim must be considered on the facts of the individual case, the judgment found: “The secretary of state erred in not considering the question of whether kidnapping amounted to exploitation.”

The ruling could help thousands of potential trafficking victims, not only those who arrive in Britain in the future but also those previously rejected as being trafficking victims who were kidnapped and held to ransom in Libya. They can now get a fresh referral into the national referral mechanism for a new decision on whether there are reasonable grounds for them to be considered a victim of trafficking.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Jeremy Bloom, a consultant solicitor at Duncan Lewis, who represented the Syrian man, welcomed the ruling. “This judgment is a vindication of my client’s claim to be a victim of trafficking. The Home Office sought to restrict the definition of exploitation,” he said.

“We hope that it will also assist other victims of trafficking who have experienced similarly horrendous treatment to gain access to the leave and support that they need to recover from those experiences. Those individuals may include people who have had their claims to be a victim of trafficking refused, and who might now be able to ask for a new decision based on the law as set out in this judgment.”

Home Office sources said it would not be appropriate to comment on legal proceedings. A Home Office spokesperson said:“We are simplifying and strengthening protection and immigration routes, ensuring they are clear, targeted and focused on their intended purpose, while maintaining fairness and public confidence.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#129. Israel’s president picks Yashar leader Gadi Eisenkot to form government

Knesset has been deadlocked since the election last month when Benjamin Netanyahu’s coalition lost its majority

Israel’s president has ⁠chosen Gadi Eisenkot, the prime minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s ⁠strongest rival, to ⁠try to ​form a new government, after a deadlocked election that left neither man ⁠with a clear path to power.

Isaac Herzog’s office said he had informed Eisenkot, the leader of the Yashar party, on Saturday night “of his intention to task him with forming a government”.

It added that the formal appointment would take place in an official ceremony on Sunday at the president’s residence in Jerusalem.

“Now is the time to form a national emergency government. I will do everything to ensure we are worthy,” Eisenkot wrote on X in his first reaction to the announcement of his designation.

Netanyahu ‌is fighting to hold on to office ‌after his religious-nationalist coalition lost its majority in an election on 27 October but still emerged as the largest bloc ‌in parliament.

Should Eisenkot succeed in forming a coalition, he would take over as prime minister from Netanyahu, Israel’s longest-serving premier.

Eisenkot’s wide-ranging opposition bloc won 48 of the Israeli parliament’s 120 seats in October elections, while Netanyahu’s camp secured 53, leaving neither with a majority.

During party consultations with Herzog, 66-year-old Eisenkot received the endorsements of both Arab-majority lists, who could now play kingmakers after making unprecedented gains in the vote.

Eisenkot faces significant challenges on the road to becoming prime minister. He has three options: a power-sharing agreement with Netanyahu, support from Arab-led parties – something several members of his bloc do not support – or an agreement with the ultra-Orthodox parties, which demand military exemptions for their community.

Eisenkot has laid out a series of conditions for any Arab party to join his coalition, including recognition of Israel as a Jewish state and declaring the Palestinian militant group Hamas a terrorist organisation. One of Eisenkot’s partners, the more conservative Yisrael Beiteinu party, already has said it will not join a coalition with Arab-led parties.

Eisenkot, a former army chief, and his partners made equal military service a central part of their campaign. Specifically, they are seeking an end to the longstanding draft exemptions enjoyed by the ultra-Orthodox.

Netanyahu has repeatedly claimed the election was “stolen” by Arab parties, which rallied their supporters to vote in large numbers. But he has offered little to no evidence to support the claims.

Netanyahu is expected to continue his pressure tactics against Eisenkot, saying any alliance with the Arab parties would be an act of betrayal.

Israel’s Shin Bet security agency has launched an investigation into possible foreign interference in the vote, but the country’s Central Elections Committee – and Herzog – say the vote was valid and even if wrongdoing was found, it did not have a significant impact on the result.

Netanyahu called for a “national unity” government with Eisenkot on Saturday night but it remains unclear whether the two rivals could agree on a power-sharing agreement; these types of coalition governments are notoriously fragile and often collapse.

Eisenkot, the military chief of staff from 2015 to 2019, has set out to unite Israelis after Netanyahu’s divisive tenure, under which the country moved sharply to the right.

He has vowed to crack down on extremist West Bank settlers who have attacked Palestinians with impunity, decrying recent incidents as “Jewish terror” abetted by Netanyahu’s government.

But he opposes the creation of a Palestinian state and vowed to end Hamas’s rule in Gaza and destroy the Islamist movement’s military wing.

Commanding the military during the 2006 war with Hezbollah, Eisenkot devised the so-called “Dahiya doctrine”. The idea, named after a Beirut suburb heavily damaged in that war, involves Israel vowing to use massive force against civilian infrastructure in areas controlled by the Lebanese militant movement Hezbollah.

After his appointment, Eisenkot will have 28 days to come up with the 61 votes needed for a government. If he comes up short, Herzog can provide a 14-day extension before he has to offer the mandate to someone else.

If neither side succeeds, Israel will head to fresh elections. The country held five elections between 2019 and 2022 owing to repeated deadlocks.

#128. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#123. Gadi Eisenkot reaches out to Netanyahu allies as he strives to form government

Former military chief says prime minister brought Israel to historic crisis and it is approaching ‘point of no return’

Israel’s former military chief Gadi Eisenkot started the tough task of forming a government on Sunday, reaching out to Benjamin Netanyahu’s allies as he said the veteran prime minister had brought the country to a historic crisis.

Eisenkot, 66, the leader of the Yashar party, who has vowed to bridge Israel’s deep polarisation under Netanyahu, formally received a mandate from the president, Isaac Herzog, who gave him 28 days to form a government despite his bloc lacking a clear majority.

“We are in a state of emergency, approaching a point of no return for the state of Israel,” said Eisenkot, who had traded his usual all black for a suit and tie at a ceremony at the presidential office. “We must form a national emergency government. This will require creative and unconventional political steps.”

Eisenkot faces significant challenges on the road to becoming prime minister. He has three options: a power-sharing agreement with Netanyahu, support from Arab-led parties – something several members of his bloc do not support – or an agreement with the ultra-Orthodox parties, which demand military exemptions for their community.

Eisenkot and Herzog acknowledged Israel’s growing isolation under Netanyahu, who is wanted by the international criminal court.

In the US, Israel’s closest ally, Democrats on Tuesday swept the midterm elections, with calls to rein in Israel after the devastation in Gaza and the unpopular Iran war, which Netanyahu launched with Donald Trump.

Eisenkot said: “We are in the midst of a devastating diplomatic crisis. Even our closest friends and strategic allies are saying their relationship with Israel must be reassessed.”

He appealed to Netanyahu’s Likud party as well as the far-right party of the finance minister, Bezalel Smotrich, to join him – but only with himself as prime minister.

“My door is open and my hand is extended, wholeheartedly and willingly, to anyone who understands the gravity of this moment,” he said.

Should Eisenkot succeed in forming a coalition, he would take over as prime minister from Netanyahu, Israel’s longest-serving premier.

Eisenkot’s wide-ranging opposition bloc won 48 of the Israeli parliament’s 120 seats in the October elections, while Netanyahu’s camp secured 53, leaving neither with a majority.

During party consultations with Herzog, Eisenkot received the endorsements of both Arab-majority lists, who could now play kingmakers after making unprecedented gains in the vote.

Eisenkot has laid out a series of conditions for any Arab party to join his coalition, including recognition of Israel as a Jewish state and declaring the Palestinian militant group Hamas a terrorist organisation. One of his partners, the more conservative Yisrael Beiteinu party, has already said it will not join a coalition with Arab-led parties.

Eisenkot, a former army chief, and his partners made equal military service a central part of their campaign. Specifically, they are seeking an end to the longstanding draft exemptions enjoyed by the ultra-Orthodox.

Netanyahu has repeatedly claimed the election was “stolen” by Arab parties, which rallied their supporters to vote in large numbers. But he has offered little to no evidence to support the claims.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Netanyahu is expected to continue his pressure tactics against Eisenkot, saying any alliance with the Arab parties would be an act of betrayal.

Israel’s Shin Bet security agency has launched an investigation into possible foreign interference in the vote, but the country’s Central Elections Committee – and Herzog – say the vote was valid and even if wrongdoing was found, it did not have a significant impact on the result.

Netanyahu called for a “national unity” government with Eisenkot on Saturday night but it remains unclear whether the two rivals could agree on a power-sharing agreement. These types of coalition governments are notoriously fragile and often collapse.

Eisenkot, the military chief of staff from 2015 to 2019, has set out to unite Israelis after Netanyahu’s divisive tenure, under which the country moved sharply to the right.

He has vowed to crack down on extremist West Bank settlers who have attacked Palestinians with impunity, decrying recent incidents as “Jewish terror” abetted by Netanyahu’s government.

He opposes the creation of a Palestinian state and has vowed to end Hamas’s rule in Gaza and destroy the Islamist movement’s military wing.

Commanding the military during the 2006 war with Hezbollah, Eisenkot devised the so-called “Dahiya doctrine”. The idea, named after a Beirut suburb heavily damaged in that war, involves Israel vowing to use massive force against civilian infrastructure in areas controlled by the Lebanese militant movement Hezbollah.

After his appointment, Eisenkot will have 28 days to come up with the 61 votes needed for a government. If he comes up short, Herzog can provide a 14-day extension before he has to offer the mandate to someone else.

If neither side succeeds, Israel will head to fresh elections. The country held five elections between 2019 and 2022 owing to repeated deadlocks.

#121. Polish prosecutors open terrorism investigation into Kraków shooting

Six people killed in attack probably inspired by far right, according to officials, as police say suspect’s parents and sister found dead at his home

Polish prosecutors have opened a terrorism investigation after a 24-year-old man opened fire in a busy market square in Kraków, killing six people including a Briton and injuring 17, in an attack that authorities said was probably inspired by far-right ideologies.

Police confirmed on Sunday that three people found dead at the gunman’s home after the attack on Saturday afternoon were his father, 53, mother, 52, and sister, 21.

As two days of national mourning began, the national prosecutor’s office said on social media it had launched an investigation into “the killing of six people … using a firearm, and the attempted murder of many others, including police officers, this act constituting a terrorist offence”.

The rare mass shooting has shocked a country that was considered safer than many of its European neighbours, and has deepened concern after several school stabbings in October that left one woman dead and which officials said were linked to extremist content online.

The Kraków suspect, a Polish national named in local media as Michal O, drove into the city’s main square in the heart of the old town, which is usually packed with tourists, at top speed at about 3pm on Saturday. Wearing military-style fatigues and a bulletproof vest, he started shooting at random as he took what the Kraków police spokesperson Katarzyna Cisło described as a “wild ride” around the square.

The interior minister, Marcin Kierwiński, said the suspect, who was apprehended and taken to hospital after being wounded in an exchange of fire with police, was fascinated with far-right ideologies and held “very radical” and “ultra-nationalistic” views.

Four women and two men were killed, including a British citizen, Polish police said. Three of the victims were Polish women. Krzysztof Klęczar, a regional official, said on Sunday that a 51-year-old Austrian woman had also been killed and the British victim was a 32-year-old man.

Five of the wounded are foreign nationals from Britain, Austria, Germany, Portugal and Russia.

Two police ⁠officers were also among the injured, one of whom was in a serious condition. The suspect underwent surgery in hospital, where he was under heavy police guard.

The bodies of the suspect’s parents and sister were found at the family home in Ostrowiec Świętokrzyski, a town about 100 miles north-east of Kraków. Police said they had probably died of gunshot wounds.

Mourners left candles in tribute to victims in the square, which was still partially cordoned off on Sunday. Flags across the country were lowered to half mast, as were European flags outside the European Commission in Brussels “in solidarity with the Polish people”, the commission said.

The Polish defence minister, Władysław Kosiniak-Kamysz, called the shooting an “act of terror” against the people of Kraków, Poland’s second largest city, which receives 15 million visitors a year, most of them passing through the main square.

Kraków authorities said the square and adjoining streets “as well as sections along the perpetrator’s escape route” would be closed for several hours. Police in Warsaw, the Polish capital, said security had been bolstered there.

Maria, a 20-year-old employee at a cafe in Warsaw, told Reuters she “almost burst into tears” when she heard about the attack in Kraków. “Recent events – not just this one, but things happening lately – are really affecting our sense of security,” ⁠she said.

The daily newspaper Rzeczpospolita said in an editorial that the mass shooting had brought a “brutal end ​to an era in which Poland was ​considered an island of safety”.

The UK prime minister, Andy Burnham, said: “I’m shocked and saddened by the appalling shooting in Kraków today. My thoughts are with the families of those who have lost their lives, everyone injured and all those affected by this senseless violence.”

The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, said his country stood in solidarity with its neighbour, calling the shooting a shocking crime.

France’s Emmanuel Macron described “shock and disbelief following the tragic shooting in Kraków”, while the German chancellor, Friedrich Merz, said: “The events in Kraków have deeply affected me,” adding that Germany stood with Poland “as a friend and partner in these difficult times”.

Italy’s prime minister, Giorgia Meloni, expressed condolences to Poland “following the vile and unacceptable act of violence”.

#119. Pope Leo urges Argentina's Milei to put people before the economy

Pope Leo XIV has urged Argentina to protect democracy and ensure economic growth benefits the poor, delivering a message to President Javier Milei over austerity, social welfare and political divisions.

Pope Leo XIV arrived in Argentina on Sunday, urging to protect democracy and ensure economic growth benefits the poor.

The pope's remarks were widely watched for criticism of President Javier Milei's harsh austerity programs and cuts affecting social services.

While Leo did not address the government's policies directly, he said "the economy must be at the service of the people, rather than people being at the service of the economy."

Talking to Milei and other government officials, he pointed to the plight of those who, "despite hard work, nonetheless struggle to meet their household's needs."

With regards to democracy, he said it was "part of Argentina's heritage that needs to be safeguarded."

The visit to Buenos Aires marks the second leg of the pope's South America tour and is the first papal visit to Argentina in nearly 40 years.

The 71-year old pontiff, who underwent surgery for lung cancer less than a month ago, arrived from Uruguay and is scheduled to visit several cities in the Catholic-majority country before continuing to Peru on Wednesday.

Milei's policies have in the past drawn strong criticism from Argentina's Catholic Church, particularly cuts affecting social services, pensions, disability assistance and public universities.

The Argentine leader, a close ally of US President Donald Trump, has defended his aggressive spending cuts as necessary to stabilize the country's ailing economy and curb inflation.

Annual inflation has fallen from more than 200% to approximately 33% under his administration, but households continue to face financial pressure, and there's been anger over public-sector layoffs and business closures.

In the past, he agressively dismissed criticsm from the Catholic Church, going as far as calling Leo's predecessor, Pope Francis, an "imbecile." He had later though softened his tone and met with Francis in Rome.

On Sunday, Milei struck a significantly more conciliatory note, praising Francis as an "exceptional figure" and the "most important Argentine in history."

Pope Leo also stressed the need to safeguard democracy in the country.

"Those who exercise authority have a duty to act with prudence, to acknowledge the limits of the power entrusted to them, and to direct their decisions toward the good of the entire community."

The Pope's words carry particular significance ahead of Argentina's presidential election in 2027, in which Milei has said he will seek another term.

The president's aggressive rhetoric and frequent attacks on journalists and political opponents are widely seen as part of an increasing political polarization in the country.

Leo told Milei that strengthening democracy required a "willingness to listen" and the "careful use of words."

His visit marks the first time a pope has traveled to Argentina since John Paul II's trip in 1987. It also brings renewed attention to the legacy of Francis, the country's first pope, who died in April 2025 without returning to his homeland during his 12-year papacy.

#116. UK spent £100m on zombie Brexit border inspection posts

Five of seven facilities in England and Wales never reached operational stage as Tories vacillated over plans to check goods

The UK wasted at least £100m in taxpayers’ money to set up zombie Brexit border inspection posts that were never used, it has been revealed.

The overall cost of Brexit was put at an estimated £4.7bn by the National Audit Office last year but this included years-long transformation of the software used for customs declarations and other systems built to cope with the UK leaving the single market.

Now fresh information from HMRC on the exact spend on the seven “inland border facilities” has come to light. The sites were built but quickly decommissioned as the Conservative government at the time vacillated over plans to check goods coming in from the continent.

Five of the seven “inland border facilities” in England and Wales never got to operational stage because the Tories decided not to implement all the border checks after concluding food coming from the EU would be the same high standard as it was before Brexit.

Portsmouth port, which is owned by the city council, spent £23m on building its border control post, which has never been used at full capacity because of the decision to scale back the checks.

The port received £17.1m in government funding for the project, leaving the council to make up the £6m shortfall, which could otherwise have been spent on essential services.

The EU launched all the checks in Calais, Dublin and other European ports from day one as the UK was treated as a “third country” overnight. The most controversial British zombie facility was known as “Dover white cliffs”. Furious residents at the village of Guston were told of the plans for the inland border facility on 31 December 2020, just hours before a “transition year” ended and Brexit was due to be implemented with supposed checks on all lorries coming across the Channel.

Information from HMRC, released under a freedom of information request, shows the facility has so far cost the taxpayer £28m, even though it remains a field. The Department for Transport paid £21m to procure the site and spent a further £7m on initial ground works, including an archaeological survey for any roman remains.

Another £17m was spent on a border facility in Birmingham planned at the airport car park. Newly released information shows it cost £11m to build with £6m in “closure costs”.

Another £20m was spent on sites in Warrington and in Ebbsfleet, a town just south of the Dartford Bridge on the Thames estuary. The Warrington site, on the former Shearings holidays coach stop at Appleton Thorn, was chosen because there were no suitable sites available for lorries coming from Ireland into Liverpool, Holyhead and Heysham ports.

Local people vehemently opposed the site at the time. It cost £7m to buy, £9m to build and £1m to decommission.

Another £10m was spent preparing customs and other checks at the former car park at Eurostar Ebbsfleet International train station, which cost £4m to convert into a lorry park and £6m to close, according to HMRC data.

A further £5m was spent on another site tucked away off the M11, just south of Stansted, at North Weald airfield. This airfield was commandeered for the same purpose, to avert congestion on the M25. It cost £3m to build and £2m to close down.

HMRC said Birmingham, Ebbsfleet and North Weald “did not require the acquisition of land”. The decision to build Brexit lorry parks so far from Dover underlined the lack of preparation for the UK’s departure from the EU.

Most of the UK’s lorry traffic is concentrated in Dover, but as a designated area of outstanding beauty it was impossible to conduct the post-Brexit customs and standard checks near the port.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

The UK finally opted for a site 20 miles inland at Sevington in Kent, but even that was last minute. The government previously revealed in a parliamentary question that total capital costs ran to £154m.

This included £30m on the acquisition of farmland between Sevington and Mersham for Britain’s principal Brexit border facility and another £70m to build.

HMRC has revealed through freedom of information that the operational costs have run to £179m, money that would not have been spent had Brexit not happened.

The second site that remains operational is Holyhead – a small site close to the main road to the port which receives goods from Ireland. According to the FoI, the site cost £14m to acquire and another £23m to build.

The figures are separate to investments made by Eurotunnel, the Port of Dover and other ports including Portsmouth. Eurotunnel spent €50m (£41.7m) on building the infrastructure for Brexit checks for trucks and €90m on changes for passengers, including an entry and exit system that has never been activated by the French.

The £23m border control post at Portsmouth, the UK’s second-busiest cross-Channel port, now faces an uncertain future. The hi-tech facility was built to government specifications to handle post-Brexit checks. Two trailer checks take place on average each day, according to the port’s director, Mike Sellers, compared with the 80 checks originally envisioned.

He said: “Given the border post’s location within the operational port, ideally we would look to repurpose it rather than demolish it, but it will cost us almost as much to repurpose as to demolish. It was bespoke and built for food import checks and biosecurity, it is not suitable for general warehousing and will have to be changed.”

Sellers and Portsmouth city council’s leader are among the ports calling on the government to provide compensation for the border posts that will become redundant when the UK government’s deal with the EU comes into force.

#115. Chinese carmaker BYD’s offices in Netherlands raided over JLR ‘copycat car’ claim

Bailiffs reportedly seized digital records before court case over alleged intellectual property breaches on Land Rover Defender

Bailiffs acting for Jaguar Land Rover raided the Dutch offices of a Chinese “copycat” rival before a legal showdown this week over whether its “Defender clone” infringes intellectual property rights.

Bailiffs acting on behalf of JLR reportedly seized digital records from the offices of Chinese carmaker BYD in Schiedam and Hoofddorp in August, according to the Sunday Times.

The raid took place before a court case in the The Hague this week which will decide whether the BYD has a case to answer over allegations that its Ti7 SUV infringes the intellectual property of the Land Rover Defender.

Motoring experts have likened BYD’s seven-seat plug-in hybrid SUV to the Defender since it went on sale in China in September last year. The company is scheduled to launch the vehicle, which the BBC’s Top Gear magazine called the “Chinese Defender clone”, in the UK market in January at a price of £47,995, undercutting the Defender by more than £20,000.

BYD says on its website that “an unapologetically boxy profile delivers the imposing, chunky design that finds favour among SUV buyers, yet delivers a contemporary look”.

The price of JLR’s seven-seat Defender 110 starts from £68,465and is one of the country’s bestselling cars. The UK-based carmaker, which is owned by India’s Tata Motors, sold 29,500 Defenders to retail customers between July and September this year, compared with 25,370a year earlier.

The raid in the Netherlands was carried out by court-ordered bailiffs, with JLR suspecting that BYD reverse-engineered some of the technology that could be used in the Ti7, the Times reported. JLR wanted to secure the digital records before they could be destroyed.

JLR and BYD declined to comment on the “ongoing legal proceedings”.

The dispute with BYD is not the first legal case JLR has pursed against a Chinese company over copycat allegations. It won a legal case to stop Jiangling Motor Corporation selling a copycat vehicle in 2019 after a three-year court battle.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

In some cases JLR has taken a collaborative approach to dealing with the rise of China’s carmakers. It agreed to form a joint venture with Chery in a 2012 deal which later included licensing its discontinued Freelander brand with the company.

Chery’s Jaecoo model, which is not part of the joint venture, has been called the Temu Range Rover because of its similarities to the JLR vehicle after years of collaboration between the pair. It costs £10,000 less than JLR’s Range Rover.

The Jaecoo was the bestselling car in the UK in October, with 10,813 registrations, taking its market share from 2.07% to 4.30%, according to the Society of Motor Manufacturers & Traders. JLR’s sales of the Range Rover fell to 43,300 in the third quarter of this year, from 47,196 cars sold in the same months last year.

#114. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”
