#112. G7 ministers meet as Europe seeks greater say in Ukraine peace talks

With Washington preparing negotiations with Moscow and Kyiv, European leaders fear Ukraine could face pressure to accept a bad deal. Iran tensions and the threat of renewed US strikes are also on the agenda.

Foreign ministers from the Group of Seven (G7) leading industrialized nations are set to meet in Paris on Monday and Tuesday for talks on how Europe can be part of US efforts to end Russia’s war on Ukraine and how to urge Washington to not resume military action against Iran.

The meeting will bring together leading diplomats from Britain, Canada, France, Germany, Italy, Japan and the United States.

It coincides with US-mediated talks between Moscow and Kyiv in Abu Dhabi scheduled to begin on Tuesday.

European governments are concerned the the US might be pushing Ukraine to a settlement favoring Russia. US President Donald Trump, in the run-up to the Abu Dhabi talks, said a "very big" deal was in the making, suggesting Ukraine could emerge from a settlement with altered ‌borders.

"I think we're gonna have it done, and I think it's ‌gonna happen ​and they're gonna end up with a little bit different shape Ukraine," Trump told broadcaster CBS. The US president also said the deal would involve all of Europe.

About the talks in Abu Dhabi, French Foreign Minister Jean-Noel Barrot told RTL radio that "Europeans will be represented and France will participate in these talks."

European leaders have long insisted that any deal to end the war should include the continent's participation in the talks and that any settlement should not come at the expense of Ukraine's sovereignty.

When asked about the US plans for a possible agreement to end the war in Ukraine, Barrot said he did not know the details but stressed that no deal affecting the European security architecture could be done without the Europeans.

Particular attention at the upcoming meeting will be on US Secretary of State Marco Rubio who will attend both days unlike the last G7 meeting where he came only for one.

Rubio suggested that recent discussions had made progress on security guarantees for Ukraine and the wider European region. Trump's envoy and son-in-law Jared Kushner has also described US efforts to develop a broader European security framework.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy has in the past expressed doubts over proposals for a new European security framework, suggesting that any such debates should follow, rather than precede, efforts to end the war.

The G7 ministers are also expected to discuss US tensions with Iran, as Trump has not ruled out additional military strikes against the country.

Washington has previously considered targeting suspected nuclear facilities, including the site known as Pickaxe Mountain, following the US midterm elections last week. Tehran denies that the location is a nuclear facility.

European governments are seeking a diplomatic resolution rather than a resumption of military action. Gobal attention is particularly on the restoration of normal shipping through the Strait of Hormuz, a critical route for international energy supplies.

Trump recently said that Iranian officials were keen to reach an agreement, suggesting negotiations remained possible.

The Paris gathering is among the final major diplomatic events of France's G7 presidency. The United States is scheduled to assume the group's rotating presidency in 2027.

#111. The young Somali woman changing how the world sees Mogadishu

Somalia has the longest coastline in mainland Africa

As the sun rises over the Indian Ocean, waves lap onto the golden sands while palm fronds rustle in the cool sea breeze. Welcome to... Mogadishu.

This is the image that one young Somali woman wants the world to have of her home city - in contrast to the one of suicide attacks and young men wielding AK-47s that many people currently associate with Somalia's capital.

Sucdi Abdul Fattah Abdul Khader is among a growing number of local guides taking visitors beyond the headlines to explore Mogadishu's beauty spots, historic sites and culture.

Her favourite spot is Jazeera beach, where visitors can enjoy the sea before heading to nearby sand dunes.

"It's one of the tourist attractions where we bring our guests. It's a nice place," says the 23-year-old.

The dunes, she adds, remind some visitors of the Sahara.

But her tours are about more than scenic views. She also takes visitors to the fish market, where they discover the variety of marine life available along Somalia's extensive coastline and to parts of the city that reflect its history as a busy Indian Ocean trading centre, with the influences of both its Ottoman and Italian former rulers.

Visitors can also sample Somali food, including camel meat, samosas and traditional tea.

Sucdi says many arrive in the city with expectations shaped by years of hearing about the insecurity.

"Mostly, they are surprised by how beautiful the country is and how welcoming people are," she says.

At the fish market, she says, visitors are particularly impressed by the variety of fish species.

Sucdi Abdul Fattah Abdul Khader wants visitors to experience the "beautiful, welcoming side of Mogadishu"

Guiding tours runs in Sucdi's family. Her father worked in the profession for over three decades, taking journalists, photographers and visitors from different countries around Somalia.

He introduced her to the business nearly three years ago.

"I started this job as a hobby, but it has become something that I like and am passionate about," she says.

She now works alongside her father and a team of guides, building on his experience while using social media to reach a wider audience.

She shares videos of her tours on TikTok, offering people outside Somalia a glimpse of places they may never have imagined visiting.

She is also considering producing content in English to reach audiences who do not speak Somali.

A wide variety of fish are available in Mogadishu's markets

Tourism remains a relatively new field in Somalia, she says, with only a handful working as guides in Mogadishu.

The business is seasonal, but Sucdi says this year has been particularly encouraging, although numbers are still fairly low. Having five tourists a week is considered a success but she is confident that numbers will grow.

However, she says many visitors are still concerned about security.

Since the collapse of Somalia's central government in 1991, Mogadishu has endured decades of civil war, destruction and displacement. Much of the city centre was reduced to rubble, and basic services and infrastructure largely broke down.

But following the withdrawal of the Islamist militant group al-Shabab from most of the city in 2011, and the establishment of the federal government in 2012, Mogadishu has undergone a gradual transformation.

Returning members of the Somali diaspora, local entrepreneurs and investors helped fuel a construction boom, with new hotels, restaurants, businesses and residential developments reshaping the capital.

Today, Mogadishu is a growing commercial hub, although insecurity and the legacy of conflict remain part of everyday life.

About 10,000 tourists visited Somalia in 2024, a 50% increase on the previous year, according to the country's tourism department.

Many visitors ask about their safety before travelling and often encounter security checkpoints around the city.

But she says their experience on the ground is generally positive.

Lido beach is one of the most popular places for people to relax in Mogadishu

"What they heard from the news and everywhere else is not the same as what they see in the country," she says.

Although some question whether Mogadishu is safe enough for a young woman to be working as a tour guide, Sucdi is determined to continue.

She wants visitors to experience a Somalia that is rarely seen.

"I would want them to see the beautiful, welcoming side of Mogadishu, and not the side that they heard from the media, which is explosions and bombs," she says.

Somalia has Africa's longest mainland coastline, stretching more than 3,000km (1,860 miles) along the Indian Ocean and Gulf of Aden.

Its beaches and sand dunes sit alongside historic coastal cities such as Mogadishu, whose culture reflects centuries of Indian Ocean trade.

For some tourists, that contrast between the country's reputation and what they experience can be striking.

American travel content creator Chris Reese, who goes by the name Authentic Travelling, has visited Mogadishu twice.

His first trip, almost a year ago, was a solo visit. He enjoyed it so much that he decided to return with a group of friends, who had seen videos of his visit on social media.

"I had such a positive experience - I really enjoyed seeing the country for the first time - that I opted to bring back a group of my friends," he says.

What stands out for Chris is the city's coastal setting, its food and atmosphere.

"It is such a beautiful tropical place. I love the vibe, the weather and the food," he says.

The sand dunes near Mogadishu remind some visitors of the Sahara Desert

During the group's visit, they enjoy lunch overlooking the Indian Ocean before heading out to explore the beaches and sand dunes.

"The desert is beautiful and picturesque. It is also amazing that it's barely a 30-minute drive from Mogadishu," he says.

Before his first trip, Chris says, much of what he knew about Somalia came from international news coverage.

"If you only read the news reports about Somalia, you would be scared to visit. And honestly, that's what I saw in the media before I came, mostly negative headlines."

But visiting the country challenged those perceptions.

"Coming here, on my very first trip, showed me that there is so much more to this country than what is shown in the headlines," he says.

Romanian Mihaela Patap says she prefers to visit new countries without preconceived ideas

Mihaela Patap, from Romania, loves Somalia's natural beauty.

Although she has spent only a few hours in Mogadishu, she has already visited the sand dunes and taken in the Indian Ocean coastline.

"The desert was so beautiful to see. I have also enjoyed the beach, and the ocean. It is inspiring," she says.

Having travelled to Somalia from neighbouring Ethiopia, she says she has been struck by the people's friendliness.

"I don't see anything dangerous. I feel safe and the people are warm," she says.

An experienced traveller, Mihaela says she prefers to visit new countries without having preconceived ideas.

"I travel the world to see new places, to see countries, to see the differences. I don't take a picture in my mind to compare the reality with. I'm just expecting the new to come to me," she says.

She hopes to share her experience and encourage others to visit Somalia.

"I'm going to post my pictures and say good things about what I'm experiencing. I'm going to promote it," she says.

She is continuing a family profession while building a business in an industry with few women and using digital platforms to reach audiences far beyond Mogadishu.

"Somalia still has a long way to go before tourism becomes a major industry," she says.

"Through my tours, I am giving visitors an opportunity to experience its landscapes, our culture and people beyond the familiar negative headlines."

For her, every visitor is another opportunity to tell a fuller story of Mogadishu.

#109. Lisa Nandy wary of ‘circular arguments’ about rejoining EU

Culture secretary pushes back against talk of revisiting Brexit but says closer ties would fulfil referendum promises

One of Andy Burnham’s cabinet ministers has said she wants to avoid “circular arguments” about whether the UK should rejoin the EU, underlining divisions at the top of government over revisiting Brexit.

Lisa Nandy, the culture secretary, appeared to suggest she was not in favour of rejoining the trade bloc, but she said the prime minister should still pursue closer ties to fulfil the promises made at the time of the referendum.

She is one of a handful of cabinet ministers who are concerned about Burnham’s plan for an “honest conversation” with the public about Brexit and who are privately warning it could reopen a row that would only benefit Nigel Farage’s Reform UK.

“I do not believe that anybody wants to repeat circular arguments about in or out, leave or remain. I do think, though, that over the last decade we have let the British people down very badly,” she told the BBC’s Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg programme.

Nandy, whose Wigan seat voted overwhelmingly for leave in 2016, and who is one of the most cautious in the cabinet about reversing Brexit, was asked if she wanted to go back in. “I don’t know if that’s a choice we have,” she said.

However, she did concede that Burnham was trying to address the failings of the Brexit deal, telling Sky News it was important to “be honest” and highlight that “the deal that we got is not what we were promised”.

The culture secretary stressed there were “trade-offs and choices” to be made which involved a conversation with people across the country, indicating that Brexit would be a key theme of the next election campaign.

Burnham will attend the European Political Community summit in Dublin on Thursday. Downing Street sources said he would use it to get Labour’s reset deal over the line and to meet many European leaders for the first time.

In private, EU counterparts will be expecting Burnham to set out his ambitions for further integration after he announced his intention to move beyond Labour’s “red lines” on the bloc after the next election.

At a Brexit reset meeting on 20 November in Brussels, agreements on agriculture and food, emissions trading and youth experience visas are expected to be confirmed.

Burnham said at the weekend there was more to come. “This summit will show what we can achieve when we work together, but it doesn’t need to be the end of our ambition,” he told the Sunday Mirror.

Officials will also discuss the agenda for the next round of the reset, which could include another attempt for the UK to join the EU’s €150bn (£125bn) Safe defence fund, collaboration on law enforcement and borders, and visas for touring artists.

Soon after the Brussels summit, Burnham is expected to give a speech setting out options for a closer relationship with the EU, arguing it would be in the UK’s long-term interest. No 10 sources said he wanted to “frame the conversation, not go straight to the endgame” before testing public opinion.

Senior government figures say Burnham wants a mandate to start negotiations with the EU in Labour’s next election manifesto, but that any subsequent referendum would be solely confirmatory.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

“You can’t ask people to vote on an undefined plan like in 2016. That was David Cameron’s mistake,” one said.

However, Burnham has faced reservations from others within his cabinet. The business secretary, Jonathan Reynolds, and home secretary, Shabana Mahmood, are cautious about pushing to fully rejoin.

The EU’s former chief Brexit negotiator Michel Barnier said London could not have the “single market à la carte” by cherrypicking which of the four freedoms – including freedom of movement – it would like in future negotiations.

“There is no cherrypicking. There will be no possibility to have one freedom and not the other,” he told the BBC.

Nandy was forced to reject allegations that celebrating the UK rejoining the Erasmus plus scheme by linking it to Remembrance Sunday was inappropriate, after suggesting it would help to honour “the immense courage of those who served our country” before Remembrance Sunday.

Kevin Hollinrake, the Tory chair, suggested she was using the national civic occasion to “try to make an EU membership pitch”. He said: “Our veterans did not fight for a student exchange scheme.”

Speaking on Sky News, Nandy responded: “They are the only party that is talking about Brexit and the EU. What we are announcing this weekend is that we’re going to widen the Erasmus scheme.

“I cannot see what is political about saying that we want young people from every part of this country to understand the contribution that their grandparents and great-grandparents made, not just to us but to many, many lives of people that they had never met,” she added.

#108. Two police officers hailed as heroes for confronting Kraków gunman

Jakub Kulik critically injured and Artur Morański also wounded after actions that ‘prevented further casualties’

Two Polish police officers who were injured as they pursued and detained the gunman in Kraków on Saturday have been hailed as heroes.

One of them is Jakub Kulik, a former paratrooper and volunteer firefighter, who was left critically injured after a close-range exchange of fire with the suspect, a 24-year-old Polish national who killed six and injured 17 in an attack believed to have been inspired by far-right ideologies.

The other injured police officer is Artur Morański. Both are under 30 years old, the Polish news network TVP World reported.

Poland’s prime minister, Donald Tusk, said on Saturday in a post on X: “They probably saved many human lives, disregarding the risk to their own.”

The Polish justice minister, Waldemar Żurek, said the rapid response and heroism of the two police officers “prevented further human casualties”.

Police arrived at the scene 23 seconds after receiving the first emergency call from Kraków’s main square, where the suspect was shooting at random from his car.

Footage that emerged on Sunday showed two police officers exchanging fire with the gunman. One of the officers then apprehended the suspect on the ground before firing his own weapon several times. The officer then fell down, apparently injured himself.

Michał Pędziwiatr and Mateusz Wierdak, two local surgeons who were having a bite to eat nearby, stepped in to help.

Wierdak “took care of the officer who subdued the attacker because he truly needed urgent help”, Pędziwiatr told the Polish news website onet.

“Without protection, without gloves, he simply focused on the officer, stopping the bleeding in his chest. This probably saved his life.”

Kulik, from the southern Polish city of Bielsko-Biala, was hit by three bullets and underwent surgery on Saturday. There has been a national appeal for blood donors.

Morański was shot in the chest area and also underwent surgery. Both men were brought out of medically induced comas on Sunday, Polskie Radio 24 reported.

Kulik is also a member of the volunteer fire service in Bielsko-Biala. “We’re very proud of him,” a volunteer firefighter from the city’s Leszczyny district told the local news website Bielsko.biala.pl. “He responds to emergencies just like we do.”

TVP World reported that Morański was from Powroźnik, a village about 90 miles (150km) south-east of Krakow and close to the border with Slovakia. A mass was held for him at the local church on Sunday.

#107. With Stephen Miller pick, Trump readies for a battle

In the aftermath of the Republican Party's sweeping defeat in Tuesday's midterm elections, Donald Trump said he would be willing to work with the newly ascendent Democrats in Congress.

On Friday, however, he elevated Stephen Miller, his bare-knuckled political operative and longtime adviser, to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff.

Far from an olive branch to Congress, Miller's promotion could be a sign that the president is girding for political battle by installing a partisan firebrand in the top White House job.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our president and defend this magnificent republic," Miller posted on X, adding that he was "honored beyond words" to be Trump's pick for the job.

One of the most powerful positions in Washington, the chief of staff is gatekeeper to the president, agenda-setter in the White House and chief negotiator with Congress and other outside political interests.

"Stephen Miller's been with me since the beginning," Trump said in comments to reporters after announcing the move on his Truth Social website. "He gets it."

Watch: Trump says he appointed Stephen Miller âto make a changeâ

Miller is best known for being the driving force behind the Trump administration's aggressive immigration enforcement policies, which has included ramping up funding for enforcement operations across the US.

He made headlines when he called Alex Pretti, who was shot and killed by federal immigration officials during a massive deportation operation in Minneapolis, a "domestic terrorist" and an "assassin". He would later walk back those words, saying federal agents "may not have been following" proper protocol.

He has also reportedly played a leading role in the US military operations in Venezuela and against suspected drug trafficking operations in the Caribbean and eastern Pacific, which critics have condemned as extra-judicial killings.

Miller joined Trump's political operation early in his 2016 presidential bid as a strategist and incendiary warm-up speaker at his raucous campaign rallies.

He was one of the few senior White House staffers to persevere through the entirety of his first term. He stayed loyal to Trump during his political exile following attempts to overturn his ballot-box defeat in 2020 failed. He then returned in the influential role of deputy chief of staff for policy in Trump's second term.

Wiles, who co-managed the president's successful 2024 re-election campaign, developed a reputation as a cool professional who avoided the White House staffing upheavals, dramatic policy reversals and personality clashes that typified Trump's first presidential term.

The president, however, began souring on Wiles in recent months, as his political standing in the US declined, culminating in Tuesday's electoral rout. He also reportedly chafed at news stories indicating that those in Wiles' orbit were landing lucrative consulting jobs through their connections to the president.

Trump names Stephen Miller as new White House chief of staff

'Trump's psyche': The aide driving president's most controversial policies

What has Stephen Millerâs role been in Trumpâs second term?

While Trump praised Miller's effectiveness as a manager on Friday, working with Congress could prove to be a challenge.

It is difficult to overstate how disliked Miller is by most Democrats â and some Republicans.

"Stephen Miller is a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands," Democratic House leader Hakeem Jeffries wrote on X. "His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor."

Earlier this year, Miller posted on social media that James Talarico, who had just secured the Democratic nomination to run for Senate in Texas, was "their first transgender Senate candidate".

The Democratic Party offered a terse, obscenity laced reply, telling Miller to shut up.

Now Miller will have to negotiate with a US Senate that includes a newly elected Talarico as one of its rising stars, after an election in which polls found that 58% of voters believe the administration's immigration enforcement actions had "gone too far" and Hispanics decisively turned against Republican candidates.

On Wednesday, in a social media post attempting to explain the Republican midterm defeats, Vice-President JD Vance - who is an early frontrunner for the Republican 2028 presidential nomination - wrote that he was "open to the idea that we should change enforcement - by focusing more on businesses that hire illegal labour, for instance".

That now seems less likely with an immigration hardline taking the White House helm.

Thom Tillis, the North Carolina Republican senator who is retiring at the end of the year, has been particularly vocal in condemning Miller. He has called him "incompetent" and "the architect of some of the dumbest proposals from the Trump White House".

Other Republicans, however, have sung his praises.

"Stephen Miller has been a true conservative warrior since his days as a Senate staffer, a staunch defender of law and order against illegal invasions and leftist lawlessness, and a steadfast fighter for President Trump for over a decade," Utah Senator Mike Lee posted on X after Trump's announcement.

At a time when congressional Democrats are promising to launch new investigations and oversight into the Trump administration, it appears that a "warrior" and a "fighter" is the kind of person the president wants to have running his White House.

#106. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#104. This man refused life-saving treatment because of his religion - days later the rules changed

Isaac Valdez had an epileptic seizure and his condition deteriorated rapidly

In August this year, 26-year-old Isaac Valdez had an epileptic seizure while browsing in his local library, causing him to convulse and strike his head against the ground.

He lost consciousness. Later, in hospital, it transpired Isaac had hit his head with such force it had led to a brain haemorrhage.

Treatment was seriously complicated by the fact that, as a Jehovah's Witness, Isaac carried a "No Blood" card, an indication that he would not accept treatment involving any form of blood transfusion because of his religious beliefs.

As a result, a procedure to drain fluid from Isaac's brain did not take place.

But his family, from Waxahachie, Texas, were encouraged by the fact that he regained consciousness and even started to talk again. He was able to confirm that he stood by his "no blood" beliefs.

The family's relief at his apparent improvement was short-lived. Isaac slipped into a coma and his condition worsened rapidly.

A neurosurgeon told Isaac's family the only way to save his life was an emergency operation, but that it was one that required blood transfusion.

His family took the decision to abide by Issac's wishes as a lifelong Witness not to accept such a procedure.

Isaac - who his twin brother Jacob Valdez describes as an intelligent man who loved volunteering and helping the elderly - died on 26 August 2026 when doctors turned off his life support.

Jacob's "no blood" card which is like the one carried in Isaac's wallet

Just 23 days later, the Jehovah's Witnesses' Governing Body announced a seismic change in teaching that would, in effect, have given Isaac the religious green light to have the life-saving treatment he had refused.

The change suddenly means the vast majority of medical procedures using donated blood are no longer prohibited to followers. Now, decisions on whether to accept such treatments are to be left to individuals.

Jacob, already dealing with the death of his twin, was angered at the Jehovah's Witnesses' leadership for not coming to their conclusion sooner. He told BBC News he believes Isaac could well still be alive if they had.

Jacob (right) believes his twin Isaac could still be alive if the change in teaching had come sooner

The original rule on blood transfusions dates back to 1945, towards the very end of a war during which the use of blood transfusions had been increasingly widespread. The leaders of Jehovah's Witnesses announced that their believers were not permitted to accept transfusions.

This was based on their interpretation of Bible verses from both the Old and New Testaments prohibiting the consumption of blood, including one from the Book of Acts that instructs believers to abstain from blood, from food offered to idols, and from things that had been strangled.

So, why has the Jehovah's Witnesses' Governing Body, based in New York state, now decided to change the rules, 81 years after introducing them?

It comes after years of pressure from both within and outside the faith, but that is not how leaders of the faith describe the decision.

"The change is basically the application of Bible principles to modern medicine and its methods," says the Jehovah's Witnesses international spokesperson, Paul Gillies, who described it as both a fresh look at scripture and at medical advances.

Jehovah's Witnesses international spokesperson Paul Gillies says the ban on transfusion of whole blood remains but blood components can now be used

We meet Gillies at the Jehovah's Witnesses UK headquarters set in 85 acres of Essex countryside. It is a vast modern complex where hundreds of volunteer followers of the faith live and work without pay.

He insists the basic teaching has not changed and that the transfusion of whole blood is still prohibited. However, for decades, most transfusions have not used whole blood, instead using the specific components of blood that are needed by the patient.

The change is being justified by permitting the use of blood components, like plasma and red blood cells and platelets, in the huge numbers of medical procedures that were previously banned.

Gillies says the Bible references "abstaining from blood" but not "various components that could be extracted from blood".

He says the Governing Body felt it their duty to avoid scriptural "overreach" and only apply what was specifically described in the Bible.

But that explanation has stunned many and led them to ask if it was not a huge overreach 81 years ago to ban blood transfusions in the first place, when transfusions are not specifically mentioned in the bible.

Others are then also asking why it has taken so long to come to this landmark decision, given that transfusions that do not use whole blood have been the norm for decades.

These are questions Jacob asked as soon as the ban was lifted, just days after a memorial service for Isaac.

Jacob, who had already left the Jehovah's Witness faith he was brought up in, texted prominent figures from Isaac's congregation in Texas after Isaac's death. The messages are full of angst, and painful to read.

He asked them to consider "the part you played in encouraging so many people to sacrifice their life for something that was never God's will".

Most did not reply, but one "elder" responded saying he could not understand why Jacob was so angry and fixated on the policy shift.

This all further infuriated Jacob, who particularly wants the faith's decision-makers to be accountable for all the lives lost.

"I want to ask them if they could apologise directly, I would like not to have a deflected answer, just straightforward apology," he says.

It is extremely difficult to say how many Jehovah's Witnesses, among the estimated nine million followers of the faith worldwide, died each year having refused treatment involving blood transfusions. But research in one medical journal in 2016, The Obstetrical & Gynecological Survey, put the figure at around 1,100.

"We're sympathetic to their feelings, and if individuals have lost family members and are concerned about some of these questions, they deserve to be heard compassionately," says Gillies.

But there was little clarity about why the change did not come earlier.

"Why has it taken this time? Well, I guess because it takes time, it doesn't necessarily mean that there is some magic change that's taken place," says Gillies.

And when asked, Gillies deflects calls for the leadership to apologise, saying that many lives had, in fact, been saved because faithful Jehovah's Witnesses had not been exposed to blood contamination scandals.

He also insists that even after the new edict, some Jehovah's Witnesses could still decide they did not want to accept a transfusion, and that may have been the case even if the edict had come years ago.

Indeed, the BBC spoke with Alex Ball, 58, a Jehovah's Witness from West Sussex, who says in 2012 a doctor told him he would not survive the chemotherapy he needed to treat his blood cancer unless he also had a transfusion.

"I wanted to adhere as closely to the scriptures as possible when I was sick, and I think I did that well."

With help from what is called "a Jehovah's Witness Hospital Liaison Committee", Alex found an American consultant who could perform a treatment to boost Alex's blood cells without the need for any products from someone else. His UK consultant agreed to treat him using the new method and it worked.

More than a decade later, Alex says he would make the same decision again, despite the change in blood policy that would have permitted the transfusions he had been told he needed.

It is not just receiving blood which has been an issue.

Elisha Cummings, 50, a Jehovah's Witness from Trinidad and Tobago, was unable to donate blood to her aunt, who died, because of the rules.

Elisha Cummings was advised by an elder not to donate blood to her aunt Eva who died

They also cost her her marriage too, with her husband of 10 years leaving the faith and divorcing her after it came to light that he had had a blood transfusion as a child, something that was looked down upon in her Witness community.

"Since the blood policy change, I have not been myself," she says. "It left me in a sort of depressed mode."

"How many houses did I go to trying to convince people about the blood doctrine? And you're coming now and it's something totally different," Elisha says.

Elisha says her beliefs as a whole have been shaken, yet she feels reluctant to leave the faith, partly because it is what provides her with her community and she fears isolation.

In the past, those who disagreed with "blood doctrine" were ostracised or felt like they needed to leave the faith. Will they now be able to come back?

"Any individual that wants to come back to the faith is warmly welcomed," says Gillies, speaking of those who had left the faith because they had accepted a transfusion for themselves or a relative.

"If they would like to become one of Jehovah's Witnesses again and would like to speak to the elders, then they're very welcome to do so."

#103. Cold blast brings springtime snow to alpine regions as temperatures plummet across Australia’s south-east

Thredbo, Perisher and Mount Hotham all recorded falls, along with Kunanyi/Mount Wellington in Tasmania

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Snow blanketed parts of New South Wales, Victoria and Tasmania across the weekend as an unseasonal cold blast whipped through Australia’s south-east, but forecasters say warm temperatures and thunderstorms are on the way.

Thredbo, Perisher and Mount Hotham all recorded falls on Saturday and Sunday, while snow also fell on Kunanyi/Mount Wellington in Hobart.

The snow had been forecast by the Bureau of Meteorology (BoM) and, though uncommon, it is not entirely unusual – many of the places hit by snow in the past 24 hours also recorded a fall in November 2022.

The cold weather isn’t expected to linger for long.

Tristan Sumarna, a meteorologist at the BoM, said lower temperatures were expected to dissipate from Tuesday, with parts of Sydney set for temperatures in the mid-20s, even the low 30s, by the end of the week.

That change will bring more of a “storm-like pattern” typical of springtime in NSW, Sumarna told ABC Radio Sydney.

Tasmania and Victoria were both set to have fine weather on Monday, with Melbourne set to hit the low 20s by lunchtime and Hobart a top of 19.

The weekend falls brought a rush of posts on social media from those puzzled by the unseasonal spring weather.

“You will not believe this, it’s springtime in Australia, in November, and it’s snowing,” Madeleine McVie, a retail manager at Thredbo, posted on Instagram on Saturday afternoon.

“Like, not just a little bit. It’s fully snowing.”

McVie posted another clip on Sunday morning of her riding empty chairlifts through the park as the snow continued to fall.

Kingston Crows cricket club in Tasmania posted an image of the Kingston Beach Oval on Saturday that showed it coated in hailstones.

The BoM said a weather system, filled with cold air moving north from towards Antarctica, had caused the wintery chill with apparent temperatures much cooler than forecast maximums. South-westerly winds were also causing the apparent or “feels like” temperature to be lower.

At midday on Sunday, Adelaide, Melbourne, Hobart, Canberra and Sydney all had temperatures that felt cooler than 13C.

Brisbane, Perth and Darwin, meanwhile, were all enjoying temperatures in the 20s or 30s at the weekend.

It may be one of the last cold snaps of the year – the BoM, as well as the UN and other bodies, have warned of the likely effects of a “jaw dropping” supercharged El Niño.

The climate crisis, caused by pollution from fossil fuels, is already supercharging extreme weather and killing people. The El Niño is expected to ratchet up the effects even further and has already begun to affect regions across the planet as weather patterns are altered.

The southern hemisphere summer is predicted to be hotter and drier as a result of the huge El Niño, meaning greater wildfire risk and droughts in Australia and southern Africa.

#100. Germans wrestle with what to do with Hitler's destroyed Eagle's Nest retreat

Fire engulfed Hitler's mountaintop Eagle's Nest retreat in Bavaria last month

Perched on a ridge high up in the spectacular scenery of the German Alps lie the burned-out ruins of a mountain house called The Eagle's Nest, which is now at the heart of an intense debate about Germany's Nazi past.

Known in German as Kehlsteinhaus, the retreat was built by the Nazis in 1938, and frequented by Adolf Hitler and other high-ranking Nazis and their guests. After World War Two it was classified as a historical monument recalling the Nazi era.

Last month it was gutted by fire in a suspected arson attack, and some believe it should be completely destroyed, the site returned to nature and all memory of its Nazi past forgotten.

But others want it either partially or completely rebuilt, wary of the risk of turning it into a site for Neo-Nazi pilgrimage at a time when Germany is witnessing the resurgence of the far-right.

Germany has an "everlasting responsibility to communicate, to deal with our Nazi past", said Isabel Heinemann, professor of contemporary history at Munich's Ludwig-Maximilians-UniversitÃ¤t (LMU).

But rebuilding the site completely, she warned, would mean putting "highly ideologised or highly ideological architecture" back in place.

Adolf Hitler used the mountain retreat to welcome guests

Before it was burned down during the evening of 23 October, the Eagle's Nest had been a popular tourist attraction, owned by the state of Bavaria and operating for many years as a restaurant.

Visitors would take a bus and then ride the original brass and mirror-panelled lift through the mountain to the Kehlsteinhaus to savour the magnificent views above the town of Berchtesgaden.

Once there, they could eat sausages in the beer garden on the Obersalzberg at 1,834m (6,017ft) above sea level, overlooking the Alps.

Unlike Hitler's nearby summer residence, the Berghof, which was bombed in World War Two and demolished in 1952, the Eagle's Nest survived the war.

Tens of thousands of tourists visited each season, providing an important source of income to the region.

In the 1990s, amid concerns that the site did not do enough to confront the crimes of the Nazi past, a museum, the Obersalzberg Documentation Centre, was opened close by.

Before it was burned down, the Kehlsteinhaus or Eagle's Nest had long been a tourist destination in Bavaria

Around 15 years ago, a photo exhibit was opened in the Kehlsteinhaus about its history.

Although the Eagle's Nest itself was "largely destroyed" by the fire, according to police, the documentation centre survived unscathed.

The authorities have not commented on a possible motive, but a 33-year-old man with a history of mental illness has been detained in a secure psychiatric hospital on suspicion of arson.

Man arrested after fire at Hitler's mountaintop Eagle's Nest retreat

Some feel this is the moment to allow the site to fade into history.

"It's been torched now. It's been destroyed. I think they should just clear away the rubble and leave the mountain as it should be in its natural state," said Prof Sir Richard J. Evans, a specialist in Nazi Germany at the University of Cambridge.

That has happened with "quite a few other Nazi sites", he argued, including the bunker in Berlin where Hitler killed himself: "The bunker was filled in with concrete and a children's playground put on the top of it on the ground."

The Kehlsteinhaus was just "a Nazi vanity project" since romanticised by the name Eagle's Nest, he said.

Police say the old Nazi retreat was largely destroyed by last month's fire

But Bavaria's authorities take a different view about the site's future.

Both the state's interior minister, Joachim Herrmann, and its minister for the arts, Markus Blume, have spoken out in favour of rebuilding the Kehlsteinhaus.

"We can hardly leave a ruin standing there," Herrmann said.

Sven Keller, head of the Obersalzberg Documentation Centre, believes it is important not to leave the site in ruins, as that could encourage Neo-Nazi pilgrims, who would be able to hike up there completely undisturbed.

"We have quite some experience at Obersalzberg with what happens if we clear those sites. Hitler's Berghof was demolished in the 1950s, and in our experience, this does not remove such sites from the mental maps of people," he told the BBC's Today programme.

Nor does he believe "a one-on-one reconstruction" of the site is possible, and Blume has made clear on Bavarian radio that "it should not and must not cost the taxpayer anything".

After Hitler's downfall, US soldiers captured the mountain-top retreat and are seen relaxing here on 4 May 1945

Herrmann said they would look for a sensible solution in consultation with historians, given the historically complex circumstances surrounding the building.

Heinemann, who also heads the Leibniz Institute for Contemporary History, believes full-scale reconstruction should be "out of the question".

"I rather think we can document the state left by the fire and also document the historical remains of the Kehlsteinhaus, and then reconstruct parts in a modern design, to make clear the different layers of time that can be seen in this building," she told the BBC.

Germany and Austria have long struggled to deal with the fraught legacy of buildings and sites connected to the Nazi era.

With the recent rise of the far-right AfD and the Austrian Freedom Party, the debate over Erinnerungskultur (Culture of Remembrance) has taken on a new urgency.

A row broke out recently in Germany when AfD and conservative CDU councillors in a town in Saxony decided to ban "stumbling stones" memorials - embedded small brass plaques in the streets - to victims of the Nazis.

In Austria over the summer, a police station was opened in the house where Hitler was born in Braunau am Inn. It followed years of controversy about how the house should be used.

The Austrian government said the police presence would help prevent neo-Nazi pilgrims.

But others had wanted the house to become a centre of responsibility, actively confronting the Nazi past.

As one resident of Braunau am Inn told me, it was a delicate balance between how much to remember and how much to forget.

#98. Wagga Wagga family members of alleged DV murderer charged with being accessories after the fact

Danielle Madgwick died in regional NSW in September after suffering multiple stab wounds to her upper body

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Family members of a man accused of the domestic violence murder of a 23-year-old woman in south-western New South Wales have been charged with being accessories to the alleged crime.

Emergency services were called to a Wagga Wagga home on the morning of 19 September where they found Danielle Madgwick suffering multiple stab wounds to her upper body. She died at the scene.

That same evening, Adam Mark Perrim, 31, who was alleged to be Madgwick’s partner, was arrested and charged with domestic violence murder. He briefly appeared in court but did not apply for bail and was remanded to reappear on 18 November.

On Monday, NSW police said they had raided on the weekend the same house where Madgwick was found. Officers arrested two men, aged 18 and 56, and a 53-year-old woman in connection with the alleged murder in mid-September.

The trio was taken to Wagga Wagga police station, with each individual charged with being an accessory after the fact to murder. They appeared in court on Saturday for a committal hearing and were refused bail to next appear on 23 December.

Court documents state those arrested on the weekend were Perrim’s brother, Bailey Perrim, his father, George Maxwell Richard Perrim, and his mother, Julie Ann Collins.

Police will allege in court all three knew Madgwick.

Her death was one of three separate cases across the state investigated as domestic violence-related homicides that weekend.

The New South Wales premier, Chris Minns, said at the time the alleged crimes were “shocking” and his thoughts were with the families and communities of the three women.

Madgwick’s father, Heath Madgwick, paid tribute to his daughter on social media last month on what would have been her 24th birthday.

“Words cannot describe the amount of emotions, thoughts and feelings that have been experienced over the last 4 weeks,” he wrote.

“There’s been enough tears to fill the world’s oceans over and over, I miss you like you wouldn’t believe, your beautiful smile, your silly laugh, our goofy video calls, the groan and eye roll I’d get with every ‘dad joke’, those are memories I’ll treasure for a lifetime.”

According to the Australian Institute of Health and Welfare, a woman was killed every 11 days in 2024-25 by her intimate partner.

#96. The Trump coalition is shattered - what will Republicans do now?

Republicans are in soul-searching mode after the coalition that powered Donald Trump to three straight presidential nominations imploded in spectacular fashion in the 2026 midterms.

The stunning election defeat immediately raised questions about the party's future post-Trump.

But there are no easy answers as Republicans face the choice of doubling down on Trumpism or trying to chart a new path forward in his final years in office.

The depth of the problem was captured by outgoing Florida Governor Ron DeSantis, who called the midterms "close to an extinction-level event for Republicans".

Others struck a more optimistic tone. Vice-President JD Vance, who blamed the midterm results on public anger over the war with Iran, argued the party could turn things around. Nevertheless Vance acknowledged the fallout will be messy.

"There will be a lot of infighting and arguing over what happened and why," Vance said.

The wide range of reactions on the right highlights deep fault lines in the party that formed around Trump's improbable rise to the presidency in 2016 and never really went away.

It's too early to tell which faction will win. But while Republicans are still licking their wounds, the intraparty battle Vance predicted is already under way.

The party must decide whether it will be "centred on one figure, one guy, or on a wider set of ideas like conservatism that can win outside the deepest red places," Rina Shah, a Republican strategist, told the BBC.

Critics on the right - the "Never Trumpers" who opposed his first White House campaign - are pushing for a return to the Republican Party's pre-Trump roots, citing the historic losses around the country on election night.

But simply turning back the clock on Trump will be extremely difficult if not impossible to do.

The party has been wholly transformed during Trump's decade-long reign. Most Republicans have shifted to the right on domestic issues like immigration, and rejected the interventionist foreign policy of the George W Bush era that led to the wars in Iraq and Afghanistan.

In Congress, the more moderate establishment wing of the party that disliked Trump from the start has almost entirely disappeared, replaced by conservative Trump backers who echo his nativism and populist rhetoric.

That trend continued this year, as old-guard Republicans like John Cornyn in Texas and Maine's Susan Collins, both of whom clashed with Trump over the years, were voted out of office.

Three days after the election Trump appointed Stephen Miller as his new White House chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

The move signalled Trump has no plans to moderate his policies or message in his remaining years in office. Miller, who had been serving as the president's chief domestic policy adviser, is a longtime Trump aide known for his hardline immigration policies.

With Stephen Miller pick, Trump readies for a battle

One of the architects of the Make America Great Again (Maga) movement, Steve Bannon, predicted a "civil war" in the party after the midterms.

Afterwards, he blamed the Republicans' defeat on the economy and "foreign wars". "I don't think we implemented the Maga programme," he said, calling on Trump to double down on mass deportations.

But other Republican strategists said the midterms were a wake up call for Make America Great Again (Maga) loyalists who want Trump's political movement to outlast his presidency.

"There's still plenty of Maga voters out there who have supported Donald Trump through thick and thin," said Whit Ayres, a Republican strategist.

"But there is also a significant portion of the Republican Party that's non-Maga, and that's a majority now."

The party lost control of the House and Senate, as well as governorships and state legislatures in red states like Texas and Kansas where Democrats had been shut out of power for decades - and where Trump won by double digits when he was on the ballot.

The stunning upsets underscored the fickle nature of political coalitions.

Trump expanded the Republican base in 2024, increasing support with Latinos and younger voters among other key groups. Republicans hailed the gains as a sign of future strength for the party.

Just two years later, the party lost ground with nearly every critical voting bloc. According to a New York Times analysis of voter data, Latino support for Democratic candidates increased by 29 percentage points from 2024-26. Support from young voters 18-29 years old increased by 33 points.

The swings reflected polling ahead of the midterms that found widespread disapproval with Trump's handling of the economy, immigration and the war with Iran.

Tuesday night saw Trump's Republicans lose in states they had comfortably held for decades

Democrats also capitalised on a public perception that the Trump family and administration is rife with self-dealing and corruption. The president's focus on the White House ballroom and other Washington construction projects also contributed to a sense among voters that he was more focused on his legacy than addressing affordability issues.

It's a sentiment shared by Benjamin Shrader, the leader of the Young Republicans in Austin, who was still shell-shocked when he spoke to the BBC in Texas the morning after his party was almost wiped out in the state by a Democratic "blue tsunami".

Shrader said Trump was still a "force of nature" when asked to reflect on how the president had used his first public appearance after the results to show off the White House ballroom project.

He hesitated before adding: "I would focus on issues that are not ballrooms, that are not renaming things. I would focus on what matters."

The evidence in the immediate aftermath of the midterms suggests that Republicans could benefit by distancing themselves from Trump ahead of 2028.

With Congress flipped, what does Donald Trump do next?

Vance tried to thread the needle with his midterm assessment blaming the Iran war and calling for a return to "America First." It was a nuanced critique that broke with Trump on an unpopular war, but embraced a broader political brand that remains popular with the Maga base.

Trump's next moves on Iran will be watched closely by the party for their 2028 implications. An escalation would risk fuelling the concerns voiced by Vance and other "America First" proponents.

In his postmortem statement on X, Vance also echoed the growing calls on the right to reexamine US support for Israel. But he also signalled an openness to making some immigration enforcement changes. That clashes with Miller, a Vance ally who has speared the administration's second-term immigration agenda.

Party strategists said the midterm drubbing complicated Vance's path to the nomination.

"Before this midterm, JD Vance was the odds-on favourite to win the Republican nomination in 2028. After this midterm, the nomination battle is completely wide open," Ayres said.

Potential Trump successors have some time to consider their options, but they'll have to make up their minds relatively soon. Any 2028 presidential hopefuls will face pressure to announce White House bids in the next few months. Some could launch campaigns before the end of the year.

Vance's election analysis was widely interpreted as an unofficial signal of his plans to run. It will likely be a crowded primary field.

Both Vice-President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio are seen as contenders for the 2028 nomination

Other rumoured White House contenders include Secretary of State Marco Rubio, Senator Ted Cruz of Texas, Florida Governor DeSantis, former US ambassador to the United Nations Nikki Haley and former New Jersey Governor Chris Christie, to name a few.

In the next election cycle, Republican presidential and congressional candidates alike will have tough decisions to make on key issues of the day, from US support for Israel to tariffs to the AI boom.

Trump's power may be diminished as he enters the final two years of his second term, but he'll still have the loudest voice in the party over the next two years. It's unclear whether he will get deeply involved in the process or take a hands-off approach.

Trump has praised Vance and Rubio at different times, but typically deflects questions about who he'd like to see succeed him.

His endorsement will loom as the biggest wildcard in the 2028 Republican primary race.

"It remains to be seen who will jump into that race," Ayres said.

"But I think it's going to be a lot larger and more diverse than we anticipated before the midterm election."

'Hopefully things get done': Americans react to historic political shift

#93. ‘I never executed Jesse Baird’: Beau Lamarre-Condon re-enacts shooting when giving evidence in NSW trial

Former police officer has pleaded not guilty to murdering Baird and Luke Davies in Paddington in early 2024

Warning: This article contains graphic content

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Beau Lamarre-Condon has told a Sydney court he did not “execute” Jesse Baird as he acted out the way he allegedly shot the 26-year-old.

The former police officer is accused of fatally shooting Baird and his new partner, Luke Davies, with his police service weapon in Baird’s Paddington home in Sydney on 19 February 2024.

He has pleaded not guilty to two counts of murder and one charge of aggravated break and enter.

Giving evidence under cross-examination on Monday, Lamarre-Condon reiterated that Baird was holding a knife while the pair struggled in the house.

Lamarre-Condon said he told the other man to drop the knife before aiming his gun at Baird’s shoulder in an attempt to disarm him.

After this shot, Lamarre-Condon’s finger remained on the trigger as both men continued to struggle, he told the New South Wales supreme court jury.

Baird then tripped and the pair fell, which led the officer to pull the trigger again, Lamarre-Condon told the court.

“That was my fault, it was unintentional,” he said on Monday.

He said the shot could have happened either during the fall or as the men impacted the ground.

Lamarre-Condon said he thought the bullet grazed the side of Baird’s head and did not know it had gone through his right temple.

The crown prosecutor Brett Hatfield SC asked whether Lamarre-Condon had “executed” Baird. Lamarre-Condon told the jury: “I never executed Jesse Baird.”

He said he believed the first shot had gone through Baird’s right shoulder.

Lamarre-Condon told the court he laid Baird on his back in the recovery position and did not look towards the shoulder area, instead searching for signs of life in his face.

Hatfield asked in court: “Did you notice there was a massive gun shot wound through [Baird’s] mouth?”

The former officer responded it was “hard to know” due to the amount of blood.

Asked if it was obvious that Baird was dead, Lamarre-Condon said: “No, I knew he was unresponsive, I just didn’t know he was dead at that point.”

Lamarre-Condon told the court he then began dialling triple zero and tried to check Baird’s pulse and get him to squeeze his hand.

The officer had entered Baird’s home dressed as an UberEats driver in a motorcycle helmet and hi-vis jacket to try retrieve his belongings from the TV presenter without anyone in the home noticing him, Lamarre-Condon told the court last week.

Hatfield on Monday asked whether Lamarre-Condon was still wearing his helmet while attempting to help Baird.

Lamarre-Condon said he did not need to take the helmet off as he was not attempting to perform CPR.

Asked if he had ever spoken to someone on the phone while wearing a motorcycle helmet, Lamarre-Condon said he could put the device on speaker phone.

The former officer told the court he then saw Davies standing in front of Baird’s bedroom door holding the firearm. Lamarre-Condon told the jury Davies said: “I’m going to fucking shoot you.”

Lamarre-Condon then got into a struggle with Davies, he told the court, grabbing his left wrist while holding the phone in his other hand as it dialled the emergency hotline.

He said on Monday that he had “trained for close to seven years” with the police to disarm people. He told the court they were taught to push the gun upwards.

“I saw his left index finger pull down on the trigger towards himself,” Lamarre-Condon told the jury.

Hatfield suggested Lamarre-Condon shot Davies through the bedroom door after hearing noise from within the bedroom. The accused denied this and claimed his ears were ringing from the first two shots which meant he could not hear Davies.

Earlier, Lamarre-Condon reenacted firing the first shot at Baird to the court after explaining Baird’s knife was next to his throat, which led him to push the other man back with his right arm.

The crown prosecutor asked Lamarre-Condon to stand up and demonstrate, telling the accused: “Put your rosary beads down while you do that.”

The officer reenacted the struggle for the jury, with Hatfield describing his movements.

“[The accused] with his left arm out at shoulder height, demonstrated pushing Jesse Baird’s right hand with the knife away from him, so that the left arm was fully extended,” the prosecutor told the jury.

“[Lamarre-Condon] gestured pulling the pistol around the area around his belt … then held it up at around chest or shoulder height and demonstrated with a bent elbow firing a shot towards the right shoulder of Jesse Baird.”

Lamarre-Condon told the court he “discharged a shot” and said he was aiming towards Baird’s right shoulder.

“My arm did extend … I don’t know to what extent … there was constant movement in this struggle,” the accused told the court. “That’s as clear as I can be. This happened very, very quick.”

Hatfield grilled the officer on the aftermath of the alleged murders, when Lamarre-Condon told a friend he had shot Baird.

He told the court he had become accustomed to “dissociating and detaching” during his time as a police officer.

“Just because you see me on camera without a reaction doesn’t mean I wasn’t crying,” Lamarre-Condon told the court.

“Jesse was someone I did care about, so I was very emotional … I’m not laughing on camera, I’m not having a good old time.”

He later told the crown it was “very interesting” the prosecution had shown the jury “30 minutes of a car sitting still” but not the entire CCTV footage of him and his friend speaking in a Sushi Train the day after the alleged murders.

“I’m not smiling, I’m not licking my fingers - I’m freaking out,” Lamarre-Condon said.

The trial before Justice Natalie Adams continues.

#84. ‘Outrageous and racist’: premier responds to NSW One Nation candidate’s claim Australians are being ‘demographically replaced’

Chris Minns and human rights commissioner lambast comments made by Stuart Bonds, who leads party’s NSW upper house ticket

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The New South Wales premier and the Australian Human Rights Commission have criticised comments by the man who heads One Nation’s upper house ticket in NSW after he said Australians faced demographic replacement unless “mass migration” was curtailed.

Chris Minns described Stuart Bonds’ comments as “frankly outrageous and racist”.

Minns said the replacement theory being espoused by Bonds was “just a conspiracy theory that’s been imported from the US here to Australia”.

Bonds will be the first candidate on the rightwing party’s NSW upper house ticket and on current polling is likely to be elected in March’s state election.

He made the incendiary remarks at an anti-immigration rally in Sydney’s CBD on Sunday. Several hundred people attended amid a sea of Australian flags.

At least one neo-Nazi, Joel Davis, was spotted among them, as well as rightwing figures including the national director of the Family First party, Lyle Shelton, who was previously head of the Australian Christian Lobby.

“Make no mistake, we are being demographically replaced inside our own nation,” Bonds told attendees after referring to abortion rates in Europe, the US and Australia.

“If we don’t support Australian mothers having Australian children, we are going to pay for other cultures’ children to be brought up in our lands.”

He warned that unless it was stopped it would be “impossible to reverse”.

Bonds told the crowd that a country was “a shared culture, a shared border, a shared flag, a shared language and a dominant religion”.

“After 240 years, we are told we’re still on stolen land, and we’re meant to apologise for being here,” he said.

“Yet, in the same vein, we’re expected to believe that an immigrant arriving yesterday is as Australian as you and me. I don’t accept that people like the Bondi shooters are as Australian as you and me. I don’t accept that.”

The race discrimination commissioner, Giridharan Sivaraman, said comments that some Australians were superior to others sowed “division and hate”.

“The idea that migrants can never truly be Australian ignores both our history and our shared future,” he said on Monday.

“Australia has always been shaped by successive generations of people who came here from around the world and helped build the nation we know today. It has the longest continuous culture in the world.

“Throughout history, periods of uncertainty have often seen minority communities cast as scapegoats. Today, migrants are too frequently portrayed as the cause of Australia’s challenges.

“Reducing complex problems to a simple narrative that migrants are to blame is not only inaccurate, it risks fuelling division, exclusion and racism. Migrants are not separate from the Australian story. They are central to it.”

Bonds told the rally that the “socialist plan” was to “destroy and bring down and tear down the fabric of your civilisation” pointing to European countries “on fire”.

He said Australia was going down a path “of becoming a special economic zone within Asia,” and that it needed to “re-establish ourselves as a proud Australian nation”.

Minns said voters should ask themselves which portfolio Bonds would have were One Nation to win the election.

“Trade, education, Treasury? I mean, what job will this bloke have in a Coalition-One Nation government?” Minns said.

“And are we going to see some distancing from those kind of views and those horrific perspectives or points of view from the Coalition?”

Comment was sought from the Coalition’s spokesperson on multiculturalism, Mark Coure, and from One Nation’s NSW headquarters.

Bonds’ comments were also reverberating in Canberra.

The federal minister for multicultural affairs, Anne Aly, said Australians with migrant backgrounds were not replacing Australians – they simply were Australians.

“Questioning someone’s right to belong because of their race or ancestry is not a debate about immigration,” she said.

“It’s divisive, dangerous and goes against everything we stand for as Australians.”

– Additional reporting by Australian Associated Press

#78. New wave of bomb threats hit European airlines

European airlines have again been hit with a new wave of bomb threats - in the third such incident this week.

On Friday multiple flights to and from Lisbon, Rome, Prague and Sofia were targeted and the threats saw three planes escorted by Hungarian fighter jets, authorities said.

It also saw a number of high-profile media organisations, including Ireland's public broadcaster RTÃ, evacuated after threats were made against them - although there has been no concrete links between the messages against airlines and the broadcasters.

The disruption comes after nearly 30 aircraft and several airports across Europe were impacted by threats earlier this week, in what the UK called a "malicious hoax".

Russian extremist group denies sending hoax bomb threats

A Ryanair flight scheduled to leave Lisbon for Rome on Friday was targeted with a threat, the airline said, while at least 11 flights were targeted in the Czech Republic and Bulgaria, according to AFP.

France also reported at least four of its flights were targeted by threats, however they all landed safely.

No explosives have been found on any planes as of Friday.

As airlines were dealing with this latest wave of threats, several media outlets, including RTÃ, were evacuated after similar warnings were made against them.

Staff at the Irish Independent newspaper and the Dublin offices of radio stations NewstalkFM and TodayFM, were also advised to leave their buildings and UK broadcaster Sky said it also received a security threat.

In a statement, gardaÃ­ (Irish police) said they were "aware of a threat made against media organisations here and in Europe".

Armed gardaÃ­ were deployed to RTÃ'S Dublin headquarter after a bomb threat

While no link has been established between the threats against airlines and media outlets, the Irish government has warned it is a "likely coordinated campaign of disruption".

In a statement, it said: "Hoax threats such as these cause fear, distress and disruption to people going about their daily lives and activities; they achieve no positive purpose whatsoever. It is not known yet who is responsible for these threats, whether it is individuals or a centralised or co-ordinated effort.

Irish Prime Minister MicheÃ¡l Martin described the situation as "worrying" and added his government's first priority was the safety of the Irish public.

"Yesterday, there appeared to be coordinated efforts to disrupt air travel," he said.

Martin told RTÃ it was not yet known if the threats had come from individuals or if it was a "coordinated attack" but said they were being taken seriously.

Ireland is scheduled to host two high-profile European Union meetings in Dublin next week.

Broadcaster Sky confirmed it had received a security threat, hours after RTÃ had evacuated its building.

The broadcaster, which is owned by Comcast, said in a statement read by a presenter on its news channel that the broadcaster had "carried out a security assessment and reported the matter to the relevant authorities".

"The safety and security of our colleagues is our primary concern, and we are working closely with authorities to ensure our people and operations remain secure."

Friday's disruption comes after the Russian extremist group "Male State", named in this week's hoax bomb threats to Italian airports, denied any link to the false warnings.

Replying to the BBC Russian Service, an account on Telegram - one of several linked to the so-called "Male State" and its founder Vladislav Pozdnyakov - mocked any suggestion the group was involved in emailing the threats.

"What Male State? Where have you seen it? It hasn't existed for many years," the message on the channel reads. "If tomorrow they sign a [threat] letter, say, from Putin, will you then try to reach our president to ask your stupid question?"

#75. Man accused of HungryPanda delivery rider’s stabbing murder yells and exposes himself in Melbourne court

Roberto Urbano, 51, appears in court via video link after death of 47-year-old Feng Jianming in the CBD on Friday night

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A food delivery rider’s alleged murderer has pulled down his pants and exposed himself as he appeared in court.

Roberto Urbano, 51, appeared in Melbourne magistrates court via video link on Monday after being charged with murder and intentionally causing serious injury.

HungryPanda rider Feng Jianming, 47, and another rider confronted Urbano after spotting him tampering with their bikes in Melbourne’s city centre on Friday night, police allege.

After a fight broke out, Urbano allegedly stabbed both riders, with Feng dying at the scene.

The second rider, 34, suffered serious injuries and remained in hospital on Monday morning.

Appearing via video link, Urbano called out, “Objection, your honour!” before saying, “I’m the one who was injured”.

The video link was muted but he continued to speak and gesture with both hands and stood up to walk around multiple times.

He then stood on the chair and pulled down his pants exposing himself to the courtroom before the link was cut.

The magistrate granted the prosecution’s application to compel Urbano to have his injury to his right arm be photographed and his DNA extracted with a mouth swab.

His lawyer confirmed his client has schizophrenia and asked for the custody nurse to assess him.

The 51-year-old was arrested by police on Saturday and on Monday morning was charged with one count of murder and intentionally causing serious injury.

Investigators were granted extra time to build their case due to multiple witnesses requiring translators and more than 30 CCTV cameras needing to be canvassed, containing hundreds of hours of footage.

Feng, originally from China’s Shaanxi province, had been in Australia for about nine years.

His wife, Xu, both their parents and two children were all financially dependent on his work.

The news of Feng’s death reverberated through Melbourne and gig worker communities in Australia and abroad.

Mourners left floral tributes and notes near the state library over the weekend.

Some cards expressed sympathy for his family, while others vented grave frustrations with messages such as “city of criminal” and “Asian lives matter”.

A GoFundMe fundraiser set up by the Melbourne Chinatown Business Association had raised almost $390,000 by Monday morning to help Feng’s family with emergency travel and funeral expenses.

The head of the Melbourne Chinatown Business Association, Christina Zhao, said Feng’s death had weighed heavily on his colleagues and gig worker communities in China, Japan and Korea.

Food delivery company HungryPanda said it was assisting the family with visa arrangements, funeral expenses, financial support and insurance.

The Transport Workers’ Union said HungryPanda needed to confirm they properly assessed risks to their workers.

“HungryPanda has legal obligations to do whatever it can to minimise risk as food delivery riders carry out their work,” the national secretary, Michael Kaine, said on Monday.

“It must urgently confirm whether it has done this, and we are calling on WorkSafe to investigate whether it has failed in its duties here, as we have seen in the past with its failure to report a workplace death.”
